### Acceptance Test Generation with Large Language Models: An Industrial Case Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07244v1)
- **Authors**: Margarida Ferreira, Luis Viegas, Joao Pascoal Faria, Bruno Lima
- **Abstract**: Large language model (LLM)-powered assistants are increasingly used for generating program code and unit tests, but their application in acceptance testing remains underexplored. To help address this gap, this paper explores the use of LLMs for generating executable acceptance tests for web applications through a two-step process: (i) generating acceptance test scenarios in natural language (in Gherkin) from user stories, and (ii) converting these scenarios into executable test scripts (in Cypress), knowing the HTML code of the pages under test. This two-step approach supports acceptance test-driven development, enhances tester control, and improves test quality. The two steps were implemented in the AutoUAT and Test Flow tools, respectively, powered by GPT-4 Turbo, and integrated into a partner company's workflow and evaluated on real-world projects. The users found the acceptance test scenarios generated by AutoUAT helpful 95% of the time, even revealing previously overlooked cases. Regarding Test Flow, 92% of the acceptance test cases generated by Test Flow were considered helpful: 60% were usable as generated, 8% required minor fixes, and 24% needed to be regenerated with additional inputs; the remaining 8% were discarded due to major issues. These results suggest that LLMs can,in fact, help improve the acceptance test process with appropriate tooling and supervision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the generation of acceptance tests for web applications. The authors propose a two-step approach: (1) AutoUAT, which generates Gherkin acceptance test scenarios in natural language from user stories, and (2) Test Flow, which converts these scenarios into executable Cypress test scripts. They integrated these tools, powered by GPT-4 Turbo, within a partner company in the automotive sector and evaluated their effectiveness. The results indicated that users found the generated acceptance test scenarios and scripts helpful in a significant number of cases (95% and 92% respectively), demonstrating the potential of LLMs to improve the acceptance testing process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a real-world problem in software testing – the labor-intensive and often overlooked task of creating and maintaining acceptance tests.
    *   **Industrial Context:**  Evaluation within an industrial setting (Critical TechWorks) gives the study significant credibility and showcases the practical applicability of the approach. This contrasts with many LLM-related papers that focus solely on academic datasets.
    *   **Two-Step Approach:** Decoupling scenario generation from script generation offers flexibility, allowing for human review and modification of scenarios before automation. This is a crucial aspect of building trust in AI-driven testing.
    *   **Quantitative and Qualitative Evaluation:** Combining quantitative data (success rates of generated tests) with qualitative user feedback provides a more complete picture of the tools' impact.
    *   **Emphasis on Tooling and Integration:** The paper doesn't just present the LLM use case, but also describes the development and integration of specific tools (AutoUAT and Test Flow), making the research more actionable.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:** Clearly articulates the problem of time-consuming AT development and proposes a structured solution with specific LLM-powered tools.
    *   **Addresses ATDD Process:** Aligns well with ATDD principles, which promotes collaboration and shared understanding between technical and non-technical stakeholders.
    *   **Detailed Architecture Description:** The flow diagrams (Figures 2 and 3) clearly outline the implementation and integration of the tools.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Sample Size:** The number of tested user stories and acceptance test cases is relatively small (especially for Test Flow), which potentially limits the generalizability of the results. They are transparent in acknowledging this, though.
    *   **Context-Specific Results:**  The evaluation is limited to a single company in the automotive sector, raising concerns about whether the findings are transferable to other industries with different development processes and product standards.
    *   **Evaluation Period:** A two-month evaluation period is somewhat short.  Longer-term studies are needed to assess the sustained impact on productivity and test quality, and to see how developers adapt to using the tools over time.
    *   **Feedback Bias:** The response rate to the feedback requests was only 40-50% of the usage, raising the possibility of selection bias (e.g., users with positive experiences might be more likely to respond).
    *   **Lack of Baseline:** The paper would have benefited from a clear baseline comparison against existing manual testing processes.  It is implied that the approach improves efficiency, but a more direct comparison would strengthen the argument.
    *   **Cost Analysis:** While they provide the cost per test script generated, a more comprehensive cost-benefit analysis, factoring in development time, maintenance costs, and potential defect reduction, would be valuable.
    *   **Threats to Validity:** This could be improved with more explicit handling of internal and external validity threats.
    *   **LLM Hallucinations:** The study doesn't deeply address the potential risks associated with LLM "hallucinations" (generating incorrect or nonsensical information). While the tools include mechanisms for review, it would be useful to discuss how such errors are detected and mitigated.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper fills a gap in the literature by exploring LLMs for acceptance testing, an area that has received less attention than unit and system testing.
    *   The industrial case study provides valuable practical insights into the challenges and opportunities of applying LLMs in a real-world setting.
    *   The two-step approach and the tooling developed (AutoUAT and Test Flow) offer a concrete framework for automating acceptance test generation.
    *   The study's focus on seamless integration within an existing workflow, incorporating feedback mechanisms and addressing data security concerns, distinguishes it from purely theoretical investigations.
    *   The results provide empirical evidence that LLMs can indeed improve the acceptance test process, especially for efficiency.

**Overall:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field, providing a practical, industry-focused exploration of LLMs for acceptance testing.  While there are some limitations in terms of sample size, context-specificity, and evaluation period, the results are promising and suggest that LLMs have the potential to significantly improve the efficiency and quality of the acceptance testing process. The clear articulation of the problem, the structured solution with specific tools, and the real-world evaluation make this a worthwhile contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution by demonstrating the practical application of LLMs for acceptance testing in an industrial setting. The development of AutoUAT and Test Flow, along with the industrial evaluation, provides useful insights into both the potential benefits and the challenges involved. While the study has limitations in terms of sample size and scope, the findings are promising and provide a foundation for future research. The practical focus and real-world results warrant a high score, but the limitations prevent it from being an exceptional contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Better Decisions through the Right Causal World Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07257v1)
- **Authors**: Elisabeth Dillies, Quentin Delfosse, Jannis Blüml, Raban Emunds, Florian Peter Busch, Kristian Kersting
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) agents have shown remarkable performances in various environments, where they can discover effective policies directly from sensory inputs. However, these agents often exploit spurious correlations in the training data, resulting in brittle behaviours that fail to generalize to new or slightly modified environments. To address this, we introduce the Causal Object-centric Model Extraction Tool (COMET), a novel algorithm designed to learn the exact interpretable causal world models (CWMs). COMET first extracts object-centric state descriptions from observations and identifies the environment's internal states related to the depicted objects' properties. Using symbolic regression, it models object-centric transitions and derives causal relationships governing object dynamics. COMET further incorporates large language models (LLMs) for semantic inference, annotating causal variables to enhance interpretability. By leveraging these capabilities, COMET constructs CWMs that align with the true causal structure of the environment, enabling agents to focus on task-relevant features. The extracted CWMs mitigate the danger of shortcuts, permitting the development of RL systems capable of better planning and decision-making across dynamic scenarios. Our results, validated in Atari environments such as Pong and Freeway, demonstrate the accuracy and robustness of COMET, highlighting its potential to bridge the gap between object-centric reasoning and causal inference in reinforcement learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COMET (Causal Object-centric Model Extraction Tool), an algorithm designed to extract interpretable causal world models (CWMs) from reinforcement learning (RL) environments. COMET operates by first extracting object-centric state descriptions from observations and identifying the environment's internal states relevant to object properties. It then uses symbolic regression to model object-centric transitions, derive causal relationships, and incorporates large language models (LLMs) for semantic inference to annotate causal variables, enhancing interpretability.  The aim is to create CWMs that align with the true causal structure of the environment, enabling RL agents to avoid spurious correlations and generalize better. The paper presents results from Atari environments like Pong and Freeway to demonstrate COMET's accuracy and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of object-centric reasoning, symbolic regression, and LLMs to extract CWMs is a relatively novel approach in the context of reinforcement learning. While object-centric RL and causal discovery have been explored individually, their integration with LLMs for enhanced interpretability represents a step forward. The use of internal states (RAM) to derive the models is interesting but limits the real-world applicability, and this aspect slightly diminishes the overall novelty as many methods avoid needing to access internal states.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in addressing the long-standing issue of brittleness and poor generalization in RL agents caused by their reliance on spurious correlations. Extracting accurate CWMs could pave the way for more robust and adaptable RL systems. However, the practical impact hinges on whether this approach can scale to more complex environments and be adapted to scenarios where access to internal states is not available. The focus on interpretability is also significant, as it aids in debugging and understanding agent behavior.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the problem of spurious correlations in RL and the need for better generalization.
    *   Comprehensive approach: COMET addresses the problem through a multi-faceted approach combining object-centric reasoning, symbolic regression, and LLMs.
    *   Empirical validation: The results in Atari environments (Pong and Freeway) demonstrate the potential of COMET in extracting accurate CWMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on internal state: The requirement of accessing the environment's internal states (RAM) limits the practical applicability of COMET to simulated environments. Most real-world scenarios do not provide such direct access.
    *   Scalability concerns: The paper does not adequately address the scalability of COMET to more complex environments with a larger state space and more objects. The computational cost of symbolic regression and LLM-based annotation could become prohibitive.
    *   Limited evaluation: The evaluation is limited to two Atari games. A more extensive evaluation across a wider range of environments would strengthen the claims.
    *   Lack of comparison with existing methods: The paper does not compare COMET's performance with existing methods for causal discovery or interpretable RL. A comparative analysis would provide a clearer understanding of COMET's strengths and weaknesses relative to the state-of-the-art.
    *   Limited exploration of the potential of LLM's common-sense reasoning capabilities: The paper touches upon using the LLM for common-sense reasoning for variable selection and interpretation but does not go into detail. It could be expanded further.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the limitations can be addressed, COMET could influence the development of more robust and interpretable RL algorithms. The idea of extracting CWMs from RL environments and using them to guide agent behavior is promising. Further development of LLM integration for common sense reasoning in this direction could make a significant difference.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel combination of techniques for addressing a significant problem in RL. However, the dependence on internal states and the limited evaluation significantly restrict its practical applicability and impact. While the ideas presented are interesting and potentially influential, the current implementation is not readily transferable to real-world scenarios. More comprehensive and rigorous evaluations are needed. The limitations and lack of a comparison with related works somewhat diminish the contribution. Thus a more conservative approach to assess its significance seems appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### RAISE: Reinforenced Adaptive Instruction Selection For Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07282v1)
- **Authors**: Lv Qingsong, Yangning Li, Zihua Lan, Zishan Xu, Jiwei Tang, Yinghui Li, Wenhao Jiang, Hai-Tao Zheng, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: In the instruction fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs), it has become a consensus that a few high-quality instructions are superior to a large number of low-quality instructions. At present, many instruction selection methods have been proposed, but most of these methods select instruction based on heuristic quality metrics, and only consider data selection before training. These designs lead to insufficient optimization of instruction fine-tuning, and fixed heuristic indicators are often difficult to optimize for specific tasks. So we designed a dynamic, task-objective-driven instruction selection framework RAISE(Reinforenced Adaptive Instruction SElection), which incorporates the entire instruction fine-tuning process into optimization, selecting instruction at each step based on the expected impact of instruction on model performance improvement. Our approach is well interpretable and has strong task-specific optimization capabilities. By modeling dynamic instruction selection as a sequential decision-making process, we use RL to train our selection strategy. Extensive experiments and result analysis prove the superiority of our method compared with other instruction selection methods. Notably, RAISE achieves superior performance by updating only 1\% of the training steps compared to full-data training, demonstrating its efficiency and effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RAISE (Reinforced Adaptive Instruction SElection), a novel framework for dynamically selecting instructions during the fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on static, heuristic-based selection methods, RAISE uses a task-objective-driven acquisition function, trained via reinforcement learning (RL), to estimate the dynamic value of each instruction at each training step. This allows the model to adapt its data needs as training progresses. The framework also incorporates a diversity constraint to ensure balanced sampling across different instruction types. Experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate that RAISE achieves superior performance compared to existing instruction selection techniques, even when using only a small fraction (1%) of the full training data. The authors emphasize the task-specific optimization capabilities of RAISE and its ability to identify high-utility data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the combination of dynamic instruction selection with reinforcement learning. Prior work has explored static instruction selection based on heuristics or difficulty-based curriculum learning, but using RL to train an acquisition function that estimates the *dynamic* value of an instruction is a significant departure. This allows for a more adaptable and task-aware training process. The emphasis on diversity constraints is also a valuable addition. While prior work explores RL for task selection in other contexts, applying it *specifically* to instruction selection within fine-tuning is a meaningful innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem in LLM fine-tuning: the need to identify and prioritize high-quality instructions to maximize model performance and efficiency. Scaling instruction datasets is costly, and the paper demonstrates that carefully selected data can often outperform full-dataset training. If the framework is robust and generalizable, it has potential to improve the fine-tuning process, reduce computational costs, and enhance the performance of LLMs on various tasks. The ability to identify *which* instructions are helpful *at which stage* of training is a valuable insight that can inform future research in curriculum learning and data selection. The 1% update claim is compelling but needs further validation across diverse datasets and architectures.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dynamic Selection:** Moving beyond static data selection is a crucial step towards more efficient and effective fine-tuning.
    *   **Task-Objective Driven:** The use of a learnable acquisition function enables task-specific optimization.
    *   **RL Framework:** Framing instruction selection as a sequential decision-making process allows for the application of powerful RL techniques.
    *   **Diversity Constraint:** Balanced sampling across instruction types helps prevent overfitting to specific patterns.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments show promising results on several benchmarks, demonstrating the superiority of RAISE over existing methods.
    *   **Analysis of Data Selection:** The ablation studies and visualizations provide insights into how RAISE selects instructions at different stages of training.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The RL training process itself introduces computational overhead. The paper should provide a more detailed analysis of the trade-off between the cost of training the acquisition function and the savings from reduced fine-tuning data.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the experiments are promising, the paper should explore the generalizability of RAISE across different datasets, model architectures, and tasks.  Does the acquisition function need to be retrained for each new task?
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** Alpaca-52K, while common, is somewhat narrow compared to the diversity seen in real-world use-cases. Testing on larger, more varied data could strengthen conclusions.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of RL algorithms can be sensitive to hyperparameter settings. The paper should discuss the robustness of RAISE to different hyperparameter choices and provide guidelines for tuning the algorithm.
    *   **Ablation Granularity:** More granular ablation of state components might be beneficial.
    *   **Memory Consumption:** The linear memory overhead mentioned in the limitation section can hinder scalability with larger and composite datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** RAISE has the potential to influence future research in instruction selection, curriculum learning, and LLM fine-tuning. The combination of dynamic selection, RL, and diversity constraints provides a solid foundation for further exploration. The framework can be extended to incorporate other factors, such as instruction difficulty or model uncertainty. The insights from this work can also inform the design of more efficient and effective fine-tuning strategies.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel and effective framework for dynamic instruction selection in LLM fine-tuning. The use of RL and a task-objective-driven acquisition function represents a significant departure from existing methods. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating the superiority of RAISE over several baselines. However, the computational overhead, limited generalizability, memory usage and hyperparameter sensitivity are concerns that need to be addressed in future work. Also the dataset diversity is limited. Weighing the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's meaningful innovation and potential impact, while acknowledging the limitations that need further investigation. The 1% result is very promising and could significantly impact how resources are allocated in training these models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MDIT: A Model-free Data Interpolation Method for Diverse Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07288v1)
- **Authors**: Yangning Li, Zihua Lan, Lv Qingsong, Yinghui Li, Hai-Tao Zheng
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied across various tasks, instruction tuning has emerged as a critical method for enhancing model performance. However, current data management strategies face substantial challenges in generating diverse and comprehensive data, restricting further improvements in model performance. To address this gap, we propose MDIT, a novel model-free data interpolation method for diverse instruction tuning, which generates varied and high-quality instruction data by performing task interpolation. Moreover, it contains diversity-based clustering strategies to ensure the diversity of the training data. Extensive experiments show that our method achieves superior performance in multiple benchmark tasks. The LLMs finetuned with MDIT show significant improvements in numerous tasks such as general question answering, math reasoning, and code generation. MDIT offers an efficient and automatic data synthetic method, generating diverse instruction data without depending on external resources while expanding the application potential of LLMs in complex environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "MDIT: A Model-free Data Interpolation Method for Diverse Instruction Tuning." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MDIT, a novel model-free data interpolation method designed to enhance the diversity of instruction tuning datasets for Large Language Models (LLMs). Addressing the limitations of existing data selection and synthesis techniques, MDIT generates varied and high-quality instruction data by interpolating between different tasks at the embedding layer. This approach aims to expand the task distribution and improve the generalization capabilities of LLMs.  MDIT also uses diversity-based clustering to filter out low-diversity data, ensuring a comprehensive and varied training dataset.  The authors demonstrate through experiments on several benchmark tasks that LLMs fine-tuned with MDIT exhibit superior performance in areas such as general question answering, math reasoning, and code generation.  MDIT offers an efficient and automatic data augmentation technique that doesn't rely on external resources, making it a cost-effective and potentially widely applicable approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** The paper directly tackles the critical issue of data diversity in instruction tuning, which is a known bottleneck for improving LLM performance.
    *   **Novel approach:** The idea of interpolating between tasks in the embedding space is a potentially valuable contribution. Leveraging the "mixup" idea from computer vision into the language space in such a way seems original and worth exploring. Using diverse tasks at the embedding level should create new, meaningful tasks.
    *   **Model-free approach:** Avoiding the reliance on external models or extensive human annotation is a significant advantage, reducing both costs and the risk of introducing biases from external sources.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The empirical results show improvements over several strong baselines on diverse benchmark tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of MDIT in enhancing LLM performance. The analysis of the clustering and ablation experiments helps give credence to the method.
    *   **Cost efficiency**: The method proposes a way of improving LLM performance without requiring additional resources.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited novelty:** Though the idea of task interpolation in the embedding space is novel *in this specific context,* mixup-style data augmentation and embedding-based methods are well-established in other areas. The real novelty lies in *how* it's applied, which needs to be emphasized and justified further.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity and Parameter Tunning**. Tuning the hyper-parameters within these models are difficult.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:** The paper lacks theoretical analysis to explain *why* this interpolation and clustering approach is effective. While the empirical results are positive, a deeper understanding of the underlying mechanisms would strengthen the paper. Also, it will be beneficial to better understand why the hyper-parameters work and test out.
    *   **Dependency between Tasks**. The models are trained to improve a task, and that task has to be improved upon. Otherwise, the interpolation will yield negative effects.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The method proposes a way of improving LLM performance without requiring additional resources.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign this paper a **Score: 6**.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a practically useful and promising method (MDIT) for enhancing data diversity in instruction tuning. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, and the model-free nature of MDIT makes it appealing in terms of cost and reduced bias.

However, the paper could be significantly strengthened by adding theoretical justification for why the method works and showing the connection from computer vision to language modeling with mixup more seamlessly. Furthermore, it needs more detail about when tasks will be interpolated on, rather than cause issues. This makes the paper a valuable contribution, but not a groundbreaking one. More analysis on the cluster would make the paper more novel.

This leads to the score of 6. A 7 would be more justifed if more justifications with the tasks were implemented and the connections were improved. A 5 if the method and results were insignificant.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Modeling Response Consistency in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: A Comparative Analysis of Shared and Separate Context Approaches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07303v1)
- **Authors**: Tooraj Helmi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly utilized in multi-agent systems (MAS) to enhance collaborative problem-solving and interactive reasoning. Recent advancements have enabled LLMs to function as autonomous agents capable of understanding complex interactions across multiple topics. However, deploying LLMs in MAS introduces challenges related to context management, response consistency, and scalability, especially when agents must operate under memory limitations and handle noisy inputs. While prior research has explored optimizing context sharing and response latency in LLM-driven MAS, these efforts often focus on either fully centralized or decentralized configurations, each with distinct trade-offs. In this paper, we develop a probabilistic framework to analyze the impact of shared versus separate context configurations on response consistency and response times in LLM-based MAS. We introduce the Response Consistency Index (RCI) as a metric to evaluate the effects of context limitations, noise, and inter-agent dependencies on system performance. Our approach differs from existing research by focusing on the interplay between memory constraints and noise management, providing insights into optimizing scalability and response times in environments with interdependent topics. Through this analysis, we offer a comprehensive understanding of how different configurations impact the efficiency of LLM-driven multi-agent systems, thereby guiding the design of more robust architectures.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of multi-agent systems (MAS) using Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on response consistency and latency.  It compares two configurations: a single agent with a shared context and multiple agents each with separate contexts.  The authors develop a probabilistic framework, introducing the Response Consistency Index (RCI) to quantify the impact of memory limitations, noise, and inter-agent dependencies.  The analysis aims to determine how these configurations affect scalability, response consistency, and overall performance in dynamic, multi-topic environments. The paper simplifies the derived models and provides visual representation of the analysis to help understand the model implication on system performance, and evaluate trade-offs between scalability, noise tolerance, and performance in shared versus separate context configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a somewhat novel perspective by explicitly framing the problem of LLM-based MAS in terms of response consistency and explicitly modeling the trade-offs between shared and separate context management. It introduces the RCI as a metric, which, while built on existing probabilistic modeling concepts, is a specific application to the LLM-based MAS context. Prior research has touched on aspects of context management and response time, but this paper explicitly focuses on quantifying consistency under different context configurations and varying memory size, which provides a new lens through which the problem of noise in MAS can be viewed.

*   **Significance:** The research addresses a crucial challenge in applying LLMs to multi-agent systems: managing context and maintaining consistent responses across interactions. The analysis sheds light on the trade-offs involved in choosing between centralized (shared context) and decentralized (separate context) approaches. The findings can inform the design of more robust and scalable LLM-driven MAS architectures. The paper lays a foundation for the understanding of response consistency which can be extended in future research to incorporate more parameters related to the interactions between multiple autonomous systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The probabilistic framework provides a structured way to analyze the impact of various factors (memory, noise, dependencies) on system performance.
    *   The RCI offers a quantifiable metric for comparing different configurations and evaluating trade-offs.
    *   The comparative analysis of shared vs. separate context approaches highlights the advantages and disadvantages of each.
    *   Model simplification and illustrative graphs contribute to greater clarity and ease of understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The model relies on several simplifying assumptions (e.g., equal topic rates, symmetric correlations) that may not hold in real-world scenarios. This limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   The model seems to focus on single turn interactions as is represented in the Response Time for Shared and Separate Contexts and may fail to incorporate complex, multi-turn interactions between agents.
    *   The empirical validation is completely absent. The paper presents a theoretical framework without any real-world experiments or simulations to validate the findings.
    *   The impact of context overflow is simplified within the probabilistic models and may fail to fully capture the complex interactions and dependencies between topics within the environment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can potentially influence the design and implementation of LLM-based MAS, particularly in scenarios where response consistency is critical. It could encourage further research into developing more sophisticated context management techniques and optimizing trade-offs between scalability, response time, and consistency.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable contribution by formalizing and quantifying the challenges surrounding response consistency in LLM-based MAS. The RCI is a helpful, albeit somewhat simplistic, metric. The weakness lies in the absence of empirical validation and the simplifying assumptions used in the model. A rigorous experimental evaluation would significantly strengthen the paper's credibility and practical value. Thus, a score of **6** is appropriate. While it provides a structured analysis and identifies key trade-offs, the lack of experimental validation and oversimplifications restrict its immediate impact and general applicability. A higher score would require a comprehensive empirical evaluation with real-world LLM-based multi-agent systems.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 6/10

### MoEDiff-SR: Mixture of Experts-Guided Diffusion Model for Region-Adaptive MRI Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07308v1)
- **Authors**: Zhe Wang, Yuhua Ru, Aladine Chetouani, Fang Chen, Fabian Bauer, Liping Zhang, Didier Hans, Rachid Jennane, Mohamed Jarraya, Yung Hsin Chen
- **Abstract**: Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) at lower field strengths (e.g., 3T) suffers from limited spatial resolution, making it challenging to capture fine anatomical details essential for clinical diagnosis and neuroimaging research. To overcome this limitation, we propose MoEDiff-SR, a Mixture of Experts (MoE)-guided diffusion model for region-adaptive MRI Super-Resolution (SR). Unlike conventional diffusion-based SR models that apply a uniform denoising process across the entire image, MoEDiff-SR dynamically selects specialized denoising experts at a fine-grained token level, ensuring region-specific adaptation and enhanced SR performance. Specifically, our approach first employs a Transformer-based feature extractor to compute multi-scale patch embeddings, capturing both global structural information and local texture details. The extracted feature embeddings are then fed into an MoE gating network, which assigns adaptive weights to multiple diffusion-based denoisers, each specializing in different brain MRI characteristics, such as centrum semiovale, sulcal and gyral cortex, and grey-white matter junction. The final output is produced by aggregating the denoised results from these specialized experts according to dynamically assigned gating probabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that MoEDiff-SR outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in terms of quantitative image quality metrics, perceptual fidelity, and computational efficiency. Difference maps from each expert further highlight their distinct specializations, confirming the effective region-specific denoising capability and the interpretability of expert contributions. Additionally, clinical evaluation validates its superior diagnostic capability in identifying subtle pathological features, emphasizing its practical relevance in clinical neuroimaging. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZWang78/MoEDiff-SR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "MoEDiff-SR," a Mixture of Experts (MoE)-guided diffusion model for region-adaptive MRI Super-Resolution (SR).  Unlike typical diffusion-based SR models, MoEDiff-SR dynamically selects specialized denoising experts at a token level, adapting to specific brain regions and enhancing SR performance. The model uses a Transformer-based feature extractor to capture multi-scale patch embeddings, which are then fed into an MoE gating network. This network assigns weights to different diffusion-based denoisers specializing in characteristics such as centrum semiovale, sulcal/gyral cortex, and grey-white matter junctions. Experimental results show that MoEDiff-SR outperforms existing SR methods regarding image quality metrics, perceptual fidelity, and computational efficiency. Furthermore, it has clinical potential, enhancing the diagnosis of subtle pathological features.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques to achieve region-adaptive SR in MRI. Specifically:
    * **MoE for SR:**  While MoE has been used in other domains, its application to MRI SR, with experts specializing in different brain regions, appears to be novel.
    * **Transformer-based feature extraction:** Using transformers for feature extraction in SR is well-established. The specific combination with multi-scale patches is an incremental improvement rather than a breakthrough.
    * **Integration of anatomical priors:** Including gradient nonlinearity and bias field corrections as input features is a valuable addition, but not fundamentally novel.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem: improving the resolution of MRI images, especially at lower field strengths, to aid in clinical diagnosis.  The reported improvements in perceptual quality and clinical utility are significant. Region-adaptive SR is also an important step, as it acknowledges the heterogeneity within MRI scans and allows for more targeted denoising.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Region-adaptive approach:** Addresses the limitations of uniform denoising strategies.
    * **Multi-scale feature extraction:**  Captures both global and local details effectively.
    * **Quantitative and qualitative results:** The paper presents both quantitative metrics and visual comparisons, strengthening the claims.
    * **Clinical validation:**  The inclusion of a clinical evaluation with real cases is a major strength. It demonstrates the practical impact of the proposed method.
    * **Computational efficiency:** The MoE approach, with asynchronous inference, contributes to computational efficiency.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental novelty:**  The paper combines existing components in a new way, rather than introducing fundamentally new techniques. While the combination is effective, the individual parts are mostly well-known.
    * **Reliance on paired data:** The method relies on paired 3T and 7T MRI data for training. Obtaining such paired datasets can be a limitation in many real-world scenarios. The generalizability to datasets acquired with different protocols or scanner manufacturers might be a concern.
    * **Limited Clinical Scope:** The clinical results are limited to one type of pathology (Multiple Sclerosis). Demonstrating efficacy across a wider range of pathologies would strengthen the clinical significance claim.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of MRI SR by promoting the use of region-adaptive techniques and demonstrating the benefits of MoE frameworks. If the method proves robust and generalizable, it could be adopted by other researchers and eventually incorporated into clinical practice.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of MRI super-resolution by intelligently combining existing deep learning methods with region-specific anatomical knowledge.  The primary strengths are the clinical validation and the demonstration of improved performance compared to state-of-the-art SR models. However, the lack of groundbreaking methodological novelty and the reliance on paired training data temper the overall impact. The paper leverages established techniques like transformers and MoE, and enhances them to achieve improved performance. The limitations regarding clinical scope also reduce the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Alice: Proactive Learning with Teacher's Demonstrations for Weak-to-Strong Generalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07316v1)
- **Authors**: Shujin Wu, Cheng Qian, Yi R., Fung, Paul Pu Liang, Heng Ji
- **Abstract**: The growing capabilities of large language models (LLMs) present a key challenge of maintaining effective human oversight. Weak-to-strong generalization (W2SG) offers a promising framework for supervising increasingly capable LLMs using weaker ones. Traditional W2SG methods rely on passive learning, where a weak teacher provides noisy demonstrations to train a strong student. This hinders students from employing their knowledge during training and reaching their full potential. In this work, we introduce Alice (pro{A}ctive {l}earning w{i}th tea{c}her's D{e}monstrations), a framework that leverages complementary knowledge between teacher and student to enhance the learning process.We probe the knowledge base of the teacher model by eliciting their uncertainty, and then use these insights together with teachers' responses as demonstrations to guide student models in self-generating improved responses for supervision. In addition, for situations with significant capability gaps between teacher and student models, we introduce cascade Alice, which employs a hierarchical training approach where weak teachers initially supervise intermediate models, who then guide stronger models in sequence. Experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly enhances the W2SG performance, yielding substantial improvements in three key tasks compared to the original W2SG: knowledge-based reasoning (+4.0%), mathematical reasoning (+22.62%), and logical reasoning (+12.11%). This highlights the effectiveness of our new W2SG paradigm that enables more robust knowledge transfer and supervision outcome.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Alice: Proactive Learning with Teacher's Demonstrations for Weak-to-Strong Generalization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Alice," a novel framework for weak-to-strong generalization (W2SG) in large language models (LLMs).  Unlike traditional W2SG methods that rely on passive learning from noisy teacher demonstrations, Alice enables proactive learning. It probes the knowledge base of a weaker teacher model by eliciting its uncertainty on a given input.  This uncertainty, along with the teacher's response, is then used to guide a stronger student model in generating improved responses for self-supervision.  The paper also presents "cascade Alice," an extension for scenarios with larger capability gaps between teacher and student, where a hierarchical training approach uses intermediate models to bridge the gap.  Experiments on knowledge-based, mathematical, and logical reasoning tasks demonstrate that Alice significantly enhances W2SG performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of proactive learning with uncertainty elicitation is a solid step beyond passive W2SG.  Leveraging the student's capabilities to improve training data is a smart approach.  The concept of Cascade Alice to handle significant capability gaps is also a valuable addition, addressing a practical limitation of standard W2SG. However, there is prior work that elicits uncertainty to improve LLM performance and/or training. Therefore, the novelty is incremental but important.

*   **Significance:** W2SG is a crucial area for scaling supervision of LLMs, particularly as models surpass human capabilities. Alice's improvements over existing W2SG methods are significant, showing potential for more robust knowledge transfer and better supervision outcomes. The focus on eliciting student knowledge during the training process, rather than solely relying on flawed teacher responses, has the potential to unlock the capabilities of models more rapidly. The performance boost on mathematical reasoning (+22.62%) is especially noteworthy, as is the improvement in logical reasoning (12.11%). The experimental results demonstrate a clear practical benefit of the proposed method. That Alice can consistently exceed the model performance when trained on ground-truth labels is an important result, even though at times by a small margin.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proactive learning approach is well-motivated and addresses a key limitation of passive W2SG.
    *   Uncertainty elicitation from teacher models is an effective way to probe knowledge gaps and guide student learning.
    *   Cascade Alice provides a practical solution for handling significant capability gaps.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements across diverse tasks.
    *   The code being made public allows for further research and adoption of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on well-articulated uncertainty from teacher models: The effectiveness of Alice depends heavily on the quality of uncertainty expression from the teacher. If the teacher is unable to accurately express its uncertainty, the framework's performance could be compromised.
    *   Single-turn learning paradigm: The current implementation uses a single-turn interaction between teacher and student. An iterative, dynamic interaction could potentially lead to even better results.
    *   Limited cascade depth: The Cascade Alice approach is limited to a two-stage process due to practical constraints. Deeper cascades could be beneficial for even larger capability gaps, but scalability is a concern.
    *   The authors' initial experiments with a two-turn interaction being "unpromising" is not investigated further, and some exploration into why that might be is missing.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of W2SG research, shifting focus towards proactive learning and leveraging student model capabilities during training. It offers a promising approach for supervising increasingly capable LLMs with limited human oversight. It will likely be influential in scaling the oversight process.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of W2SG. The proactive learning approach and cascade Alice extension are valuable advancements. While the core idea might not be entirely revolutionary (uncertainty already used elsewhere in LLM literature), the specific application in W2SG and the significant performance improvements justify a positive assessment. The weaknesses are acknowledged and provide clear directions for future research. Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contribution and potential impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Zeus: Zero-shot LLM Instruction for Union Segmentation in Multimodal Medical Imaging
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07336v1)
- **Authors**: Siyuan Dai, Kai Ye, Guodong Liu, Haoteng Tang, Liang Zhan
- **Abstract**: Medical image segmentation has achieved remarkable success through the continuous advancement of UNet-based and Transformer-based foundation backbones. However, clinical diagnosis in the real world often requires integrating domain knowledge, especially textual information. Conducting multimodal learning involves visual and text modalities shown as a solution, but collecting paired vision-language datasets is expensive and time-consuming, posing significant challenges. Inspired by the superior ability in numerous cross-modal tasks for Large Language Models (LLMs), we proposed a novel Vision-LLM union framework to address the issues. Specifically, we introduce frozen LLMs for zero-shot instruction generation based on corresponding medical images, imitating the radiology scanning and report generation process. {To better approximate real-world diagnostic processes}, we generate more precise text instruction from multimodal radiology images (e.g., T1-w or T2-w MRI and CT). Based on the impressive ability of semantic understanding and rich knowledge of LLMs. This process emphasizes extracting special features from different modalities and reunion the information for the ultimate clinical diagnostic. With generated text instruction, our proposed union segmentation framework can handle multimodal segmentation without prior collected vision-language datasets. To evaluate our proposed method, we conduct comprehensive experiments with influential baselines, the statistical results and the visualized case study demonstrate the superiority of our novel method.}
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Zeus: Zero-shot LLM Instruction for Union Segmentation in Multimodal Medical Imaging":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Zeus, a novel end-to-end framework for union segmentation in multimodal medical imaging.  It aims to address the challenge of limited paired vision-language datasets in the medical domain by leveraging the zero-shot capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework works by using a frozen LLM to generate text instructions based on the input medical images (e.g., MRI, CT), mimicking the radiology scanning and report generation process. These generated instructions, along with the image embeddings, are then fed into a mask decoder (from Segment Anything Model or SAM) for final segmentation.  The authors evaluate their method on three public multimodal medical image datasets (MSD-Prostate, MSD-Brain, and CHAOS) and compare it against several established segmentation baselines. Results show the superiority of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by combining LLMs for instruction generation with the SAM-based segmentation for multimodal medical imaging.  The novelty lies primarily in the *application* of zero-shot instruction generation from LLMs to this specific problem, rather than in groundbreaking LLM architectures or segmentation techniques themselves. Zero-shot instruction generation, although promising, is not entirely new. A key aspect is the emphasis on "union segmentation," aiming to realistically model the diagnostic process where multiple modalities are considered.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its potential to reduce the reliance on expensive and hard-to-obtain paired vision-language medical datasets.  If the zero-shot approach proves robust and generalizable, it could significantly accelerate progress in multimodal medical image analysis. The realistic modeling of diagnosis using combined modalities makes the method closer to a real-world situation than the traditional domain shift settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a very important and practical problem in medical imaging -  segmentation with limited labeled multimodal data.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The approach is clearly explained, with well-defined components and a logical flow.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors conduct thorough experiments on multiple datasets and with various baselines.
    *   **Good Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed Zeus framework outperforms several competitive baselines.
    *   **Ablation Study:**  The ablation studies provides insights into the importance of different components of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited LLM Exploration:** While the paper mentions different LLMs, it focuses heavily on a specific LLM (Vicuna).  A more thorough investigation into the impact of different LLM architectures and pre-training strategies would be valuable. The tuning process for knowledge spaces alignment has the possibility for overfitting.
    *   **Dependence on MedCLIP:** The method relies heavily on MedCLIP for vision encoding and instruction prompt encoding.  This introduces a dependency on a specific pre-trained model. A more modular framework that could accommodate different vision encoders and instruction encoders would be more robust.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements over existing methods, while statistically significant, aren't always dramatic, especially on certain datasets. This suggests that the zero-shot instruction generation is not a magic bullet, but rather a valuable addition.
    *   **Zero-shot claim:** It is more "few-shot" because MedClip is pre-trained in medical images and texts. If the LLM is completely out-of-distribution, Zeus might not work very well.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the framework proves to be easily adaptable to new modalities and segmentation tasks, it could influence the development of more data-efficient medical image analysis pipelines.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper provides a good step in an important direction. It offers a useful method to address the problem of dataset limitation for multimodal medical image segmentation. However, there are several limitations as listed above, so it isn't a breakthrough and the experiments could be further enhanced.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Code Generation with Small Language Models: A Deep Evaluation on Codeforces
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07343v1)
- **Authors**: Débora Souza, Rohit Gheyi, Lucas Albuquerque, Gustavo Soares, Márcio Ribeiro
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated capabilities in code generation, potentially boosting developer productivity. However, their widespread adoption remains limited by high computational costs, significant energy demands, and security risks such as data leakage and adversarial attacks. As a lighter-weight alternative, Small Language Models (SLMs) offer faster inference, lower deployment overhead, and better adaptability to domain-specific tasks, making them an attractive option for real-world applications. While prior research has benchmarked LLMs on competitive programming tasks, such evaluations often focus narrowly on metrics like Elo scores or pass rates, overlooking deeper insights into model behavior, failure patterns, and problem diversity. Furthermore, the potential of SLMs to tackle complex tasks such as competitive programming remains underexplored. In this study, we benchmark five open SLMs - LLAMA 3.2 3B, GEMMA 2 9B, GEMMA 3 12B, DEEPSEEK-R1 14B, and PHI-4 14B - across 280 Codeforces problems spanning Elo ratings from 800 to 2100 and covering 36 distinct topics. All models were tasked with generating Python solutions. PHI-4 14B achieved the best performance among SLMs, with a pass@3 of 63.6%, approaching the proprietary O3-MINI-HIGH (86.8%). In addition, we evaluated PHI-4 14B on C++ and found that combining outputs from both Python and C++ increases its aggregated pass@3 to 73.6%. A qualitative analysis of PHI-4 14B's incorrect outputs revealed that some failures were due to minor implementation issues - such as handling edge cases or correcting variable initialization - rather than deeper reasoning flaws.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper benchmarks the performance of five open-source Small Language Models (SLMs) – LLAMA 3.2 3B, GEMMA 2 9B, GEMMA 3 12B, DEEPSEEK-R1 14B, and PHI-4 14B – on 280 competitive programming problems from the Codeforces platform. The models were tasked with generating Python code solutions, and their performance was evaluated using pass@k, topical accuracy, semantic consistency, and error analysis. The study finds that PHI-4 14B significantly outperforms the other SLMs, achieving a pass@3 of 63.6%.  It also explores the impact of using C++ with PHI-4 14B, finding that combining Python and C++ solutions increases the aggregated pass@3 to 73.6%.  The authors also conduct a qualitative analysis of PHI-4 14B's errors, revealing that many failures stem from minor implementation issues rather than fundamental reasoning flaws. The paper concludes that SLMs like PHI-4 14B offer a viable, cost-effective alternative to large proprietary models for code generation tasks, particularly with a multi-language approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper offers a fairly comprehensive evaluation of SLMs on a relevant and challenging task (competitive programming). The use of Codeforces problems provides a good benchmark that is tied to real-world software development skills.  The range of problem difficulties and topics is also a strength.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper goes beyond simple pass@k metrics. It includes an analysis of error types, topical accuracy, and semantic consistency, which provides a richer understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of each model.  The qualitative error analysis adds significant value by identifying specific failure modes.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The study highlights the potential of SLMs as cost-effective alternatives to larger proprietary models, which is particularly relevant given the increasing concerns about the computational cost and accessibility of LLMs. The analysis regarding the complementary strengths of Python and C++ is also insightful and practical.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors explicitly state that data and code are publicly available (though the link must be accessed to be assessed directly), which is crucial for reproducibility and future research.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The paper describes the experimental setup, prompting strategies, and evaluation metrics clearly, ensuring transparency and facilitating replication.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the evaluation is thorough, the core methodology of benchmarking models on Codeforces problems is not entirely novel.  Prior works (cited in the paper) have also used this platform.  The novelty primarily lies in the specific set of SLMs evaluated and the depth of the analysis.
    *   **Focus on Pass@k:** Although the paper acknowledges its limitations, the core evaluation still relies heavily on pass@k, which can be a somewhat coarse metric.  More sophisticated metrics that assess code quality (e.g., code complexity, readability, style) could have been included, though this would also increase the workload significantly.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Details:** While the paper mentions the prompt design, it doesn't provide detailed examples of the prompts used. This would further enhance reproducibility and allow other researchers to understand the specific prompting strategies employed.
    *   **Generalizability:** While Codeforces is a useful platform, it mainly tests algorithmic and data structure skills. The extent to which these results generalize to other code generation tasks (e.g., web development, application programming) is not fully addressed.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Review:**  The qualitative review only examines 21 problems, and while insights are valuable, a larger sample size would strengthen the confidence.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper provides valuable empirical evidence that supports the growing interest in SLMs for code generation. By demonstrating the capabilities of PHI-4 14B and highlighting the benefits of a multi-language approach, the study contributes to a more nuanced understanding of the tradeoffs between model size, performance, and cost-effectiveness. The error analysis offers actionable insights for improving SLM performance in code generation.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The thoroughness of the evaluation, the insightful analysis of error types, and the practical implications of using SLMs are significant strengths. The multi-language aspect is a key takeaway, providing a concrete strategy for improved performance.
*   However, the paper's limited novelty in terms of methodology, reliance on pass@k as a primary metric, limited details of the prompts, and questions about generalizability temper the score.

The paper represents a solid contribution that advances our understanding of SLMs in code generation and provides practical guidance for developers and researchers.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Throughput-Optimal Scheduling Algorithms for LLM Inference and AI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07347v1)
- **Authors**: Yueying Li, Jim Dai, Tianyi Peng
- **Abstract**: As demand for Large Language Models (LLMs) and AI agents rapidly grows, optimizing systems for efficient LLM inference becomes critical. While significant efforts have targeted system-level engineering, little is explored through a mathematical modeling and queuing perspective. In this paper, we aim to develop the queuing fundamentals for LLM inference, bridging the gap between queuing and LLM system communities. In particular, we study the throughput aspect in LLM inference systems. We prove that a large class of 'work-conserving' scheduling algorithms can achieve maximum throughput for both individual requests and AI agent workloads, highlighting 'work-conserving' as a key design principle in practice. Evaluations of real-world systems show that Orca and Sarathi-serve are throughput-optimal, reassuring practitioners, while FastTransformer and vanilla vLLM are not maximally stable and should be used with caution. Our results highlight the substantial benefits queuing community can offer in improving LLM inference systems and call for more interdisciplinary developments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) inference systems for throughput. It develops a queuing-theoretic framework to model LLM inference, explicitly capturing the prefill and decode phases and batching.  The core result demonstrates that work-conserving scheduling algorithms are throughput-optimal for single-instance LLM inference. The paper analyzes popular LLM serving systems (FasterTransformer, vLLM, Orca, and Sarathi-Serve) within this framework, validating the throughput optimality of Sarathi-Serve and Orca and highlighting the throughput limitations of FasterTransformer and vLLM. It also extends the analysis to AI-agent workloads, revealing scenarios where even work-conserving policies can fail to achieve optimal throughput in networked LLM systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of queuing theory to the analysis and design of LLM inference systems is a significant contribution. While both queuing theory and LLM serving systems are well-established areas, the integration of these areas to understand and optimize LLM inference performance is novel. It provides a mathematical foundation for understanding the throughput capabilities of these systems, which has been largely driven by empirical observations thus far.The analytical modeling of LLM-specific challenges like dual-phase processing and dynamic batch formation is also a novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The identification of work-conserving scheduling as a throughput-optimal principle offers practical guidance for practitioners designing LLM serving systems.  The analysis of existing systems like FasterTransformer, vLLM, Orca, and Sarathi-Serve, validating some and critiquing others provides valuable insights and reassurance to practitioners in the space.The extension of the framework to AI-agent workflows, even though limited to homogeneous models, is timely and addresses a growing area of LLM applications. The paper highlights challenges in this multi-agent setting that would not be apparent without a formal model, hinting at fruitful research in the future.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Mathematical Foundation:**  The paper introduces a formal queuing-theoretic framework which strengthens the foundation of the work.
    *   **Practical Implications:**  The findings about work-conserving scheduling and the analysis of existing LLM serving systems have immediate practical implications for system design and selection.
    *   **Extension to AI Agents:** The paper expands the scope to AI-agent workloads, addressing a relevant and rapidly evolving area.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The work analyzes the existing systems in light of their throughput guarantees.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplifications:**  Like any analytical model, the paper makes several simplifications. While the model captures key aspects of LLM inference, it does not address all complexities of real-world deployments such as heterogeneous hardware, more complex agent workflows, KV cache memory constraints, or the impact of system-level optimizations on the workload models derived. The assumption of discrete time and the piecewise linear batch processing time model, although reasonable, are approximations.
    *   **Limited AI-Agent Analysis:** The analysis of AI-agent workloads, while valuable, is limited to homogeneous LLM engines and idealized network scenarios. The fork-join model is also relatively simple.
    *   **Limited Latency Analysis:** While the paper shows the significance of throughput as a first-order concern, more robust guarantees on latency and more comprehensive study of how latency constraints affect algorithm design is lacking.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a solid mathematical framework for understanding throughput in LLM inference systems and offers practical insights for system design. While the model contains some simplifying assumptions and the AI-agent analysis could be more extensive, the work represents a significant step towards a more principled approach to LLM serving system optimization. The identification of work-conserving policies and validation of existing systems is valuable, and the initial exploration of the AI-agent domain points toward important future research directions. The rigorous proofs and the connection between the theory and existing systems bolsters its significance in practice.

### Revisiting Prompt Optimization with Large Reasoning Models-A Case Study on Event Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07357v1)
- **Authors**: Saurabh Srivastava, Ziyu Yao
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) such as DeepSeek-R1 and OpenAI o1 have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in various reasoning tasks. Their strong capability to generate and reason over intermediate thoughts has also led to arguments that they may no longer require extensive prompt engineering or optimization to interpret human instructions and produce accurate outputs. In this work, we aim to systematically study this open question, using the structured task of event extraction for a case study. We experimented with two LRMs (DeepSeek-R1 and o1) and two general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) (GPT-4o and GPT-4.5), when they were used as task models or prompt optimizers. Our results show that on tasks as complicated as event extraction, LRMs as task models still benefit from prompt optimization, and that using LRMs as prompt optimizers yields more effective prompts. Finally, we provide an error analysis of common errors made by LRMs and highlight the stability and consistency of LRMs in refining task instructions and event guidelines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper systematically investigates the impact of prompt optimization on Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) such as DeepSeek-R1 and OpenAI o1, focusing on the complex structured task of event extraction (EE).  The authors experiment with two LRMs (DeepSeek-R1 and o1) and two general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) (GPT-40 and GPT-4.5), using them both as task models (Mtask) and prompt optimizers (Mopt) within a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) framework.  The key findings are: (1) LRMs benefit from prompt optimization, even though their intrinsic reasoning capabilities are high. (2) LRMs scale more consistently than LLMs across optimization depths. (3) LRMs generate better prompts when used as optimizers, often creating shorter, more precise prompts with extraction rules and exception cases mirroring human annotation guidelines. (4) LRMs facilitate more efficient and stable optimization, achieving convergence at shallower MCTS depths with lower variance.  The paper includes error analyses to identify common errors and offers insights into the stability and consistency of LRMs in refining task instructions and event guidelines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic empirical study of prompt optimization's effects on LRMs, specifically within the context of structured information extraction. Prior work has explored prompt optimization for LLMs in various tasks. The paper's central claim is to check if the same is true for LRMs or if their reasoning capabilities make prompt optimization unnecessary. In particular, most research is in tasks where zero-shot capabilities are already quite impressive, whereas event extraction as an information extraction task poses significant challenges. The comparison between LRMs and LLMs as *both* task solvers and prompt optimizers is a useful experimental design.

*   **Significance:**  The findings are significant because they challenge the assumption that LRMs are inherently immune to the need for prompt engineering. The demonstration that even these powerful models benefit substantially from optimized prompts has important practical implications for how we deploy and utilize LRMs in real-world applications. The insights into the types of prompts LRMs generate as optimizers and their stable behavior in the MCTS framework provides a good understanding of their properties and potential utility.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Design:** The paper uses a well-controlled experimental setup, comparing LRMs and LLMs under various conditions (different optimizers, MCTS depths, dataset sizes).
    *   **Relevant Task Selection:** Event extraction is a challenging structured prediction task that effectively reveals differences in model performance.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper includes thorough error analysis, providing qualitative insights into the types of errors made and the impact of prompt optimization on specific error categories.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper offers insights into prompt optimization based on the results, for example, concise prompts that avoid extraneous phrasing

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Scope:** The study is focused on the ACE05 dataset (and a subset thereof), which, although widely used, may not fully generalize to other domains or event extraction tasks. The downsampling of ACE05 to 10 event types could raise concerns of representativeness.
    *   **Black-box Optimizers:** The paper treats the optimizer LLMs as black boxes. While the quantitative analysis is strong, a deeper qualitative analysis of the prompt optimization strategies adopted by different LRMs and LLMs (e.g., more specific vs. more general rules) would provide valuable insights. This is partially addressed in section 4.1 and table 2, but could be expanded.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The resource demands of MCTS are considerable. The paper could discuss scalability issues more thoroughly, such as how the MCTS depth and batch size impact compute costs, and whether alternatives like heuristic-based prompt optimization or gradient-based tuning are viable given the computational cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways: (1) It motivates further investigation into task-specific optimization strategies for LRMs. (2) It encourages exploration of alternative prompt optimization techniques that are more scalable and computationally efficient. (3) It suggests the potential for LRMs to be used as "meta-optimizers" to improve prompts for other models.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically studying prompt optimization for LRMs in a challenging structured prediction task.  The findings are well-supported by experimental evidence and offer practical insights for researchers and practitioners working with LRMs.  The main weakness is the dataset.
