### GPT Carry-On: Training Foundation Model for Customization Could Be Simple, Scalable and Affordable
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07513v1)
- **Authors**: Jianqiao Wangni
- **Abstract**: Modern large language foundation models (LLM) have now entered the daily lives of millions of users. We ask a natural question whether it is possible to customize LLM for every user or every task. From system and industrial economy consideration, general continue-training or fine-tuning still require substantial computation and memory of training GPU nodes, whereas most inference nodes under deployment, possibly with lower-end GPUs, are configured to make forward pass fastest possible. We propose a framework to take full advantages of existing LLMs and systems of online service. We train an additional branch of transformer blocks on the final-layer embedding of pretrained LLMs, which is the base, then a carry-on module merge the base models to compose a customized LLM. We can mix multiple layers, or multiple LLMs specialized in different domains such as chat, coding, math, to form a new mixture of LLM that best fit a new task. As the base model don't need to update parameters, we are able to outsource most computation of the training job on inference nodes, and only train a lightweight carry-on on training nodes, where we consume less than 1GB GPU memory to train a 100M carry-on layer on 30B LLM. We tested Qwen and DeepSeek opensourced models for continue-pretraining and got faster loss convergence. We use it to improve solving math questions with extremely small computation and model size, with 1000 data samples of chain-of-thoughts, and as small as 1 MB parameters of two layer layer carry-on, and the results are promising.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a "GPT Carry-On" framework for customizing large language models (LLMs) efficiently. Instead of retraining or fine-tuning the entire LLM for each user or task, the authors suggest training a lightweight adapter module (the "carry-on") on top of the final layer embeddings of a pre-trained, frozen base LLM. This approach aims to reduce the computational and memory requirements for customization, allowing training to be performed even on resource-constrained inference nodes. The carry-on module can be a simple linear transformation, a mixture-of-experts (MoE) transformer, or a more complex architecture. The authors explore techniques to balance customization with maintaining general intelligence, including a rescale factor to control the contribution of the carry-on module.  They present experiments with Qwen and DeepSeek models, demonstrating faster loss convergence and improved performance on math problem-solving tasks with very small parameter sizes (around 1MB).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of attaching a small, trainable module to a frozen LLM for customization is not entirely novel. Parameter-efficient transfer learning techniques like LoRA already exist.  However, this paper distinguishes itself by its emphasis on *extreme* efficiency, explicitly targeting deployment scenarios where even fine-tuning a LoRA adaptor on inference nodes might be too demanding. The approach of offloading most computation to inference nodes while training only a tiny carry-on on separate training nodes is also a practical contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to make LLM customization more accessible and scalable, particularly for scenarios with limited computational resources or numerous users requiring personalized models.  If the proposed framework proves robust across diverse tasks and base models, it could lower the barrier to entry for customized LLM applications, leading to wider adoption.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Extreme Efficiency Focus:** The paper's strong focus on minimizing computational and memory requirements for training is valuable.
    *   **Practical Deployment Scenario:** The work directly addresses the practical challenge of deploying customized LLMs in real-world inference environments.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments with Qwen and DeepSeek models, showing faster convergence and improved performance on GSM8K tasks are promising, although the scope of the experiments is relatively limited.
    *   **Balance Customization with Generalization:** Techniques for balancing customization and retaining the general knowledge of the base LLM are key to the success of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The experiments are primarily focused on a few tasks (pretraining with certain datasets, GSM8K). A more comprehensive evaluation across a wider range of tasks (e.g., text summarization, translation, creative writing) is needed to assess the robustness of the framework. Also, the dataset size of 1000 examples is relatively small for training an LLM.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** The paper suggests the framework is scalable, but lacks in-depth analysis of the practical limits of scaling to millions of users and tasks.
    *   **Comparisons to Existing Methods:** While acknowledging LoRA, the paper could benefit from a more direct comparison with other parameter-efficient transfer learning techniques, particularly in terms of resource requirements and performance trade-offs.
    *   **Generalization:**  The paper needs to discuss how well it performs on a variety of LLMs. Currently, the paper is only tested with Qwen and DeepSeek.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical framework provided seems a bit disjointed. The VC dimension discussion feels out of place, and more rigorous justification of the performance is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's approach has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and scalable LLM customization techniques. If the framework proves effective in real-world deployments, it could inspire further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a potentially valuable framework for efficient LLM customization, particularly targeting resource-constrained environments. However, the limited scope of evaluation, the scalability concerns, and the lack of a more rigorous theoretical analysis and comparison to existing methods temper its significance.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Enhancements for Developing a Comprehensive AI Fairness Assessment Standard
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07516v1)
- **Authors**: Avinash Agarwal, Mayashankar Kumar, Manisha J. Nene
- **Abstract**: As AI systems increasingly influence critical sectors like telecommunications, finance, healthcare, and public services, ensuring fairness in decision-making is essential to prevent biased or unjust outcomes that disproportionately affect vulnerable entities or result in adverse impacts. This need is particularly pressing as the industry approaches the 6G era, where AI will drive complex functions like autonomous network management and hyper-personalized services. The TEC Standard for Fairness Assessment and Rating of AI Systems provides guidelines for evaluating fairness in AI, focusing primarily on tabular data and supervised learning models. However, as AI applications diversify, this standard requires enhancement to strengthen its impact and broaden its applicability. This paper proposes an expansion of the TEC Standard to include fairness assessments for images, unstructured text, and generative AI, including large language models, ensuring a more comprehensive approach that keeps pace with evolving AI technologies. By incorporating these dimensions, the enhanced framework will promote responsible and trustworthy AI deployment across various sectors.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancements for Developing a Comprehensive AI Fairness Assessment Standard".

**Summary:**

The paper argues that the existing TEC Standard for Fairness Assessment and Rating of Artificial Intelligence Systems, while a good starting point, is limited because it primarily focuses on tabular data and supervised learning models.  The paper proposes enhancements to broaden the standard's applicability by including fairness assessments for images, unstructured text, and generative AI models, particularly Large Language Models (LLMs). It outlines specific techniques and considerations for assessing and mitigating biases in each of these modalities. The goal is to ensure the TEC Standard remains relevant and effective in promoting responsible and trustworthy AI deployment across various sectors, especially in the context of the evolving 6G telecommunications landscape.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Real Need:** The paper correctly identifies a gap in existing AI fairness assessment frameworks. As AI systems become more complex and are deployed across various data types, the existing focus on structured data becomes inadequate.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper effectively highlights unique bias considerations in images, unstructured text, and LLMs. The suggested enhancements are tailored to the specific challenges posed by each modality, indicating a thorough understanding of the issues.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The proposals for incorporating specific assessment techniques (e.g., Tabular Reduction Techniques for images, WEAT/SEAT for text, probability-based metrics for LLMs) offer concrete steps that can be implemented. This makes the paper practically relevant for developers and organizations using the TEC Standard.
    *   **Contextual Relevance:** The framing of the problem within the context of the 6G telecommunications era adds relevance and highlights the importance of fairness in emerging technologies.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper proposes valuable enhancements, the novelty is arguably incremental. The suggested techniques are, for the most part, adaptations of existing methods used in fairness research for different data types. The paper does not introduce fundamentally new fairness metrics or assessment methodologies.
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper is primarily theoretical and does not include empirical validation of the proposed enhancements. It does not demonstrate the effectiveness of the suggested techniques in a practical setting. This limits its immediate impact and makes it difficult to assess the actual benefits of the proposed additions to the TEC Standard.
    *   **Limited Depth:** While covering a wide range of modalities, the paper necessarily lacks depth in each. The discussions of bias assessment techniques for each modality are relatively brief and do not delve into the nuances and limitations of each method.
    *   **Scope of Applicability:** While aiming for comprehensiveness, the paper may not address fairness concerns across the board. For example, time-series analysis might need different considerations for fairness which is untouched.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Impact on Standard Development:** The paper has the potential to influence the future development and evolution of the TEC Standard, making it more relevant and comprehensive.
    *   **Promotion of Responsible AI:** By highlighting the importance of fairness across diverse AI modalities, the paper contributes to the broader effort of promoting responsible AI development and deployment.
    *   **Practical Guidance:** The paper provides practical guidance for organizations seeking to assess and mitigate biases in their AI systems, particularly in the context of emerging technologies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying the limitations of an existing AI fairness standard and proposing specific enhancements to broaden its scope. However, the novelty is limited, and the lack of empirical validation detracts from its immediate impact. While the paper offers practical guidance, the discussions are relatively brief, and the overall depth could be improved. The influence of this paper would be largely dependent on its eventual incorporation into the TEC Standard, and its subsequent adoption by the community. Taking the positive and negative aspects into account, the paper provides a valuable extension to an existing framework; however, the lack of novel research findings and empirical validation limits its contribution.

### VideoExpert: Augmented LLM for Temporal-Sensitive Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07519v1)
- **Authors**: Henghao Zhao, Ge-Peng Ji, Rui Yan, Huan Xiong, Zechao Li
- **Abstract**: The core challenge in video understanding lies in perceiving dynamic content changes over time. However, multimodal large language models struggle with temporal-sensitive video tasks, which requires generating timestamps to mark the occurrence of specific events. Existing strategies require MLLMs to generate absolute or relative timestamps directly. We have observed that those MLLMs tend to rely more on language patterns than visual cues when generating timestamps, affecting their performance. To address this problem, we propose VideoExpert, a general-purpose MLLM suitable for several temporal-sensitive video tasks. Inspired by the expert concept, VideoExpert integrates two parallel modules: the Temporal Expert and the Spatial Expert. The Temporal Expert is responsible for modeling time sequences and performing temporal grounding. It processes high-frame-rate yet compressed tokens to capture dynamic variations in videos and includes a lightweight prediction head for precise event localization. The Spatial Expert focuses on content detail analysis and instruction following. It handles specially designed spatial tokens and language input, aiming to generate content-related responses. These two experts collaborate seamlessly via a special token, ensuring coordinated temporal grounding and content generation. Notably, the Temporal and Spatial Experts maintain independent parameter sets. By offloading temporal grounding from content generation, VideoExpert prevents text pattern biases in timestamp predictions. Moreover, we introduce a Spatial Compress module to obtain spatial tokens. This module filters and compresses patch tokens while preserving key information, delivering compact yet detail-rich input for the Spatial Expert. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and versatility of the VideoExpert.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoExpert, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed to improve performance on temporal-sensitive video understanding tasks. The key idea is to address the limitations of existing MLLMs by decoupling temporal perception and content generation into two specialized modules: the Temporal Expert and the Spatial Expert. The Temporal Expert processes high-frame-rate, compressed tokens to capture dynamic changes and perform temporal grounding, while the Spatial Expert focuses on content detail analysis using spatial tokens and language input. A Spatial Compress module filters and compresses patch tokens to provide detail-rich input to the Spatial Expert without excessive computational cost. The two experts collaborate through a special token indicating event localization. Experiments on four benchmarks (Charades-STA, QVHighlights, YouCookII, and NextGQA) demonstrate the effectiveness and versatility of VideoExpert across tasks such as temporal grounding, highlight detection, dense video captioning, and grounding question answering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem in MLLMs for video understanding: the difficulty in handling temporal information and the tendency to rely on language patterns rather than visual cues for timestamp predictions. The core idea of separating temporal perception and content generation into specialized modules is sound and has the potential to improve performance.

*   **Novelty:** The modular architecture with Temporal and Spatial Experts, coupled with the Spatial Compress module, introduces a novel approach to video understanding with MLLMs. This separation allows for specialized training and prevents text-pattern biases, which is a genuine contribution. However, the individual components, like LoRA adapters and CLIP encoders, are not entirely novel, but their specific integration and application in this context are unique.

*   **Significance:** The experimental results demonstrate that VideoExpert achieves remarkable results, surpassing existing MLLM-based methods and even competing with task-specific models on multiple benchmarks. This suggests the approach is significant and has the potential to advance the state-of-the-art in temporal-sensitive video understanding. The comprehensive evaluations and ablation studies are thorough.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated solution.
    *   Novel architecture with specialized modules for temporal and spatial reasoning.
    *   Spatial Compress module reduces computational cost without sacrificing performance.
    *   Extensive experiments and ablation studies to demonstrate effectiveness and justify design choices.
    *   The improvement over existing MLLM methods is substantial on several datasets.
    *   The paper emphasizes and justifies decoupling timestamp generation from text, addressing limitations of existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper argues for parameter decoupling to prevent text pattern biases, it would be useful to show this more explicitly, for instance, with a detailed analysis of the model's behaviour under different query styles or visual conditions.
    *   The integration strategy using a dedicated `<LOC>` token may introduce architectural complexity. Further simplification could be explored.
    *   While the paper offers comprehensive experimental results, more detailed analysis of failure cases is warranted. A better understanding of where the approach struggles may further inform future improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's architecture and results could influence future research in MLLMs for video understanding by encouraging modular designs and specialized modules for different aspects of the task. The idea of decoupling temporal reasoning from content generation could also be applied to other multimodal tasks.

*   **Room for Improvement:**
    *   Deeper analysis of failure cases and how different types of questions and videos affect performance.
    *   Exploring more sophisticated fusion methods between the Temporal and Spatial Experts.
    *   Investigating the transferability of VideoExpert to other video-related tasks.

The design choice may require additional computational cost, and more efficient implementations could be explored. The analysis of the ablation results would benefit from more in-depth discussions of the impact of different components on specific types of tasks. Also the authors could have included more comparisons with specialized algorithms on each particular task, i.e., dense captioning and grounding question answering.

Score: 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and effective approach to temporal-sensitive video understanding using MLLMs. The modular architecture and Spatial Compress module are valuable contributions, and the experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods. The thorough evaluation and ablation studies strengthen the claims. However, the paper has a few weaknesses, such as a detailed analysis of failure cases and further improvements to justify the module's presence, keeping it from a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Why We Feel: Breaking Boundaries in Emotional Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07521v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxiang Lin, Jingdong Sun, Zhi-Qi Cheng, Jue Wang, Haomin Liang, Zebang Cheng, Yifei Dong, Jun-Yan He, Xiaojiang Peng, Xian-Sheng Hua
- **Abstract**: Most existing emotion analysis emphasizes which emotion arises (e.g., happy, sad, angry) but neglects the deeper why. We propose Emotion Interpretation (EI), focusing on causal factors-whether explicit (e.g., observable objects, interpersonal interactions) or implicit (e.g., cultural context, off-screen events)-that drive emotional responses. Unlike traditional emotion recognition, EI tasks require reasoning about triggers instead of mere labeling. To facilitate EI research, we present EIBench, a large-scale benchmark encompassing 1,615 basic EI samples and 50 complex EI samples featuring multifaceted emotions. Each instance demands rationale-based explanations rather than straightforward categorization. We further propose a Coarse-to-Fine Self-Ask (CFSA) annotation pipeline, which guides Vision-Language Models (VLLMs) through iterative question-answer rounds to yield high-quality labels at scale. Extensive evaluations on open-source and proprietary large language models under four experimental settings reveal consistent performance gaps-especially for more intricate scenarios-underscoring EI's potential to enrich empathetic, context-aware AI applications. Our benchmark and methods are publicly available at: https://github.com/Lum1104/EIBench, offering a foundation for advanced multimodal causal analysis and next-generation affective computing.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can summarize and critically evaluate the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task called "Emotion Interpretation" (EI), which aims to go beyond simply recognizing emotions in images and instead focuses on *why* a person is experiencing a particular emotion.  It argues that current emotion analysis methods are limited by their focus on emotion labels and neglect the causal factors driving those emotions. To facilitate research in EI, the authors present EIBench, a new multimodal benchmark dataset with both basic and complex scenarios. The complex scenarios involve multilayered emotions and nuanced contextual factors.  They propose a Coarse-to-Fine Self-Ask (CFSA) annotation pipeline to generate high-quality labels at scale.  The paper evaluates the performance of various open-source and proprietary large language models (LLMs) under different settings and shows that there are consistent performance gaps, particularly for intricate scenarios, highlighting the potential of EI to improve empathetic and context-aware AI applications. The dataset and code are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of moving beyond emotion recognition to emotion interpretation is novel and significant. Emotion recognition, while useful, often lacks the depth needed for truly empathetic AI. Focusing on causal factors opens up new avenues for creating more context-aware and human-like AI systems. The shift in emphasis from *what* to *why* is a crucial step forward in affective computing.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to impact several areas, including human-computer interaction, mental health, and user experience research.  The EIBench dataset provides a valuable resource for researchers to explore EI and develop new models.  The demonstration of performance gaps in existing LLMs highlights the need for further research in this area.  Furthermore, the introduction of the CFSA pipeline provides a practical method for creating high-quality datasets for complex reasoning tasks involving multimodal data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing emotion recognition methods and defines the EI task effectively.
    *   **High-quality dataset:** EIBench is a valuable resource, with both basic and complex scenarios. The detailed annotation and the explanation of the CFSA pipeline enhances the resource.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experimental setup is well-designed, with multiple evaluation modes, open-source and closed-source models. This provides a thorough analysis of the current state-of-the-art.
    *   **Publicly available dataset and code:** This promotes reproducibility and further research in the area.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scale of complex EI samples:** While the basic EI samples are of reasonable quantity, only 50 complex EI samples could limit more fine-grained analyses of complex scenarios.
    *   **Subjectivity of Emotion Interpretation:**  Emotion interpretation is inherently subjective. Although the authors employ a Coarse-to-Fine Self-Ask (CFSA) method for their annotations, subjectivity from the annotators might still creep in, potentially affecting the dataset's validity and generalizability.
    *   **Evaluation metrics**: Emotional trigger recall is a good initial metric; however, it might not fully capture the quality of the generated explanation, and the coherence measure might be more general and less specific to the emotion interpretation task. The quality of generated emotional triggers might also need to be evaluated directly.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of affective computing by introducing the Emotion Interpretation task and providing a valuable resource for research in this area. The EIBench dataset and the CFSA pipeline offer a solid foundation for future work on building more empathetic and context-aware AI systems. While there are some limitations in the scale and subjectivity of the data, the overall impact of the paper is substantial.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper tackles a crucial and largely unaddressed problem in affective computing. It introduces a novel task, provides a significant dataset, and offers a practical annotation method. The identified limitations, particularly concerning the scale of complex scenarios and the subjective nature of emotion interpretation, prevent it from achieving a higher score. However, the strong definition of the task, quality of the dataset, and comprehensive evaluation justify a score of 8, indicating a significant contribution with potential for substantial impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Supervised Optimism Correction: Be Confident When LLMs Are Sure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07527v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Zhang, Rushuai Yang, Shunyu Liu, Ting-En Lin, Fei Huang, Yi Chen, Yongbin Li, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: In this work, we establish a novel theoretical connection between supervised fine-tuning and offline reinforcement learning under the token-level Markov decision process, revealing that large language models indeed learn an implicit $Q$-function for inference. Through this theoretical lens, we demonstrate that the widely used beam search method suffers from unacceptable over-optimism, where inference errors are inevitably amplified due to inflated $Q$-value estimations of suboptimal steps. To address this limitation, we propose Supervised Optimism Correction(SOC), which introduces a simple yet effective auxiliary loss for token-level $Q$-value estimations during supervised fine-tuning. Specifically, the auxiliary loss employs implicit value regularization to boost model confidence in expert-demonstrated responses, thereby suppressing over-optimism toward insufficiently supervised responses. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, including GSM8K, MATH, and GAOKAO, showcase the superiority of the proposed SOC with beam search across a series of open-source models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of over-optimism in beam search when used with Large Language Models (LLMs) fine-tuned via Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). The authors establish a theoretical connection between SFT and offline Reinforcement Learning (RL), demonstrating that LLMs learn an implicit Q-function. They argue that beam search, which relies on this implicit Q-function, often amplifies errors due to overestimated Q-values, especially in insufficiently supervised states. To mitigate this, they propose Supervised Optimism Correction (SOC), an auxiliary loss applied during SFT to boost model confidence in expert-demonstrated responses and suppress over-optimism towards insufficiently supervised responses. They demonstrate the effectiveness of SOC on mathematical reasoning benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, GAOKAO) with several open-source models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The theoretical connection between SFT and offline RL, specifically highlighting the implicit Q-function learning in LLMs during SFT, is a significant contribution. The identification and formalization of the over-optimism problem in beam search within this framework are also novel. While techniques exist to prevent over-optimism in reinforcement learning (RL) settings, the adaptation and application of similar insights to the SFT paradigm, where direct reward modeling is not the norm, is relatively novel. The SOC loss is itself fairly simple. However, the justification from the perspective of implicit Q-function learning is novel. Prior work have investigated beam search performance, but often from a practical perspective rather than the analytical approach presented in this paper.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important challenge in deploying LLMs: improving reasoning capabilities, especially in resource-constrained scenarios. The over-optimism problem in beam search can severely degrade performance. The proposed SOC method offers a lightweight yet effective solution, requiring no architectural changes or inference-time computations. The experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements in accuracy on mathematical reasoning tasks, which are critical benchmarks for evaluating LLM reasoning. The improvements for different model sizes are also encouraging. The connection to offline RL provides theoretical support for the observed improvement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a well-defined theoretical framework for understanding the over-optimism problem in beam search for LLMs.
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The motivation for addressing the over-optimism problem and aligning training objectives with inference-time goals is clear and well-articulated.
    *   **Simplicity and Effectiveness:** The proposed SOC method is simple to implement and computationally efficient, making it easily adoptable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments on multiple datasets and models provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of SOC.
    *   **Code Availability:**  The promise of publicly available code enhances reproducibility and allows others to build upon the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. While important, it would be valuable to demonstrate the effectiveness of SOC on other types of reasoning tasks and in different domains (e.g., code generation, question answering).
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of SOC depends on the tuning hyperparameter λ.  A sensitivity analysis or adaptive tuning method could further strengthen the approach.
    *   **Comparison with Other RL Fine-tuning Methods:** The paper would benefit from a more direct comparison with methods that require explicit reward function or verifier during fine-tuning to better understand the trade-offs in terms of computation and effectiveness. The paper notes that SFT with auxiliary losses does not require any external reward models or verifiers, which is beneficial.
    *   **Over-reliance on sparsity Assumption**: The assumption that intermediate rewards is zero is a strong one, and may not apply generally.
    *   **Presentation issues**: The authors do not define what an expert demonstration is.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field by highlighting the importance of aligning training objectives with inference-time goals in LLMs. The SOC method offers a practical solution to the over-optimism problem in beam search, which could lead to improved performance in various LLM applications. The theoretical analysis can also inspire further research on the relationship between SFT and RL in LLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**
The paper makes a novel theoretical and practical contribution to the field of LLMs, specifically addressing a critical problem in beam search. The theoretical framework, the simple yet effective SOC method, and the strong empirical validation make it a valuable contribution. However, the limited scope of the experiments and the need for hyperparameter tuning slightly detract from its overall impact. Addressing these weaknesses in future work could further solidify the paper's contribution.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automating the Path: An R&D Agenda for Human-Centered AI and Visualization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07529v1)
- **Authors**: Niklas Elmqvist, Clemens Nylandsted Klokmose
- **Abstract**: The emergence of generative AI, large language models (LLMs), and foundation models is fundamentally reshaping computer science, and visualization and visual analytics are no exception. We present a systematic framework for understanding how human-centered AI (HCAI) can transform the visualization discipline. Our framework maps four key HCAI tool capabilities -- amplify, augment, empower, and enhance -- onto the four phases of visual sensemaking: view, explore, schematize, and report. For each combination, we review existing tools, envision future possibilities, identify challenges and pitfalls, and examine ethical considerations. This design space can serve as an R\&D agenda for both visualization researchers and practitioners to integrate AI into their work as well as understanding how visualization can support HCAI research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Automating the Path: An R&D Agenda for Human-Centered AI and Visualization" proposes a systematic framework for integrating Human-Centered AI (HCAI) into the visualization discipline. It maps four key HCAI capabilities (amplify, augment, empower, enhance) onto the four phases of visual sensemaking (prepare, explore, schematize, report). For each combination, the authors review existing tools, envision future possibilities, identify challenges, and examine ethical considerations.  The paper serves as a research and development agenda for researchers and practitioners to integrate AI into their visualization work, and also examines how visualization can support HCAI research.  The authors argue against creating a new discipline, instead advocating for a course correction within the existing visualization field leveraging HCAI principles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and timely topic, given the rapid advancements in AI and their potential impact on visualization. The framework directly addresses the question of integrating AI into visualization, which is a major concern in the field.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The four-by-four matrix framework (HCAI capabilities vs. sensemaking phases) provides a structured and comprehensive way to analyze the potential of AI in visualization.
    *   **Practical Examples and Considerations:**  The inclusion of current HCAI tools, challenges, pitfalls, and ethical considerations within each cell of the framework adds significant practical value.  This grounding in real-world concerns makes the paper more impactful.
    *   **Builds on Established Work:** The paper makes explicit reference to, and builds upon, previous work, particularly "Illuminating the Path," which positions this paper as a natural evolution of the field's research agenda.
    *   **Strong call for democratizing AI:**  The push for making AI accessible and empowering for domain experts is a crucial aspect that resonates with broader trends of human-AI collaboration.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth in Ethical Considerations:** While the paper acknowledges ethical concerns, the depth of analysis in each cell is somewhat limited. Greater discussion of specific ethical issues (e.g., bias amplification in specific visualization types, privacy in automated reporting) would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of Novel Methods or Algorithms:**  The paper primarily offers a conceptual framework and does not introduce novel visualization techniques or AI algorithms. Its value lies in its organization and perspective rather than technical innovation.
    *   **Relatively High-Level Overview:** The discussion of each combination (HCAI capability + sensemaking phase) remains at a relatively high level.  Concrete examples of new visualization designs or interaction techniques enabled by HCAI are somewhat lacking.
    *   **Potentially U.S. Centric.**  It is important to expand the HCAI definition to include international collaboration across different cultures and societies.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The paper's novelty stems from its comprehensive framework for systematically exploring the intersection of HCAI and visualization. While individual applications of AI in visualization exist, this paper provides a structured way to think about and organize the potential of this integration across the entire sensemaking pipeline.
    *   The linking of existing visualization phases to HCAI concepts provides a novel and helpful way to think about research and development in the area.
*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper offers a valuable roadmap for future research in visualization and HCAI. It can guide researchers in identifying promising research directions, practitioners in adopting AI-enhanced visualization tools, and policymakers in addressing the ethical implications of this integration.
    *   It fosters a conversation about the ethical and societal impact of automating the analysis process, making researchers aware of their importance as developers.

**Overall Score:**

The paper offers a timely and valuable contribution to the field by providing a structured framework for integrating HCAI into visualization. While it lacks novel technical contributions and could benefit from deeper ethical analysis, its comprehensive perspective and practical considerations make it a significant contribution. It serves as a solid foundation for future research and development in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### A taxonomy of epistemic injustice in the context of AI and the case for generative hermeneutical erasure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07531v1)
- **Authors**: Warmhold Jan Thomas Mollema
- **Abstract**: Whether related to machine learning models' epistemic opacity, algorithmic classification systems' discriminatory automation of testimonial prejudice, the distortion of human beliefs via the 'hallucinations' of generative AI, the inclusion of the global South in global AI governance, the execution of bureaucratic violence via algorithmic systems, or located in the interaction with conversational artificial agents epistemic injustice related to AI is a growing concern. Based on a proposed general taxonomy of epistemic injustice, this paper first sketches a taxonomy of the types of epistemic injustice in the context of AI, relying on the work of scholars from the fields of philosophy of technology, political philosophy and social epistemology. Secondly, an additional perspective on epistemic injustice in the context of AI: generative hermeneutical erasure. I argue that this injustice that can come about through the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) and contend that generative AI, when being deployed outside of its Western space of conception, can have effects of conceptual erasure, particularly in the epistemic domain, followed by forms of conceptual disruption caused by a mismatch between AI system and the interlocutor in terms of conceptual frameworks. AI systems' 'view from nowhere' epistemically inferiorizes non-Western epistemologies and thereby contributes to the erosion of their epistemic particulars, gradually contributing to hermeneutical erasure. This work's relevance lies in proposal of a taxonomy that allows epistemic injustices to be mapped in the AI domain and the proposal of a novel form of AI-related epistemic injustice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "A taxonomy of epistemic injustice in the context of AI and the case for generative hermeneutical erasure" aims to provide a structured framework for understanding and addressing epistemic injustices related to Artificial Intelligence (AI). It begins by reviewing existing literature on various forms of epistemic injustice (testimonial, hermeneutical, participatory, contributory, etc.) and integrating them into a general taxonomy. The paper then extends this taxonomy to the AI domain, highlighting how AI systems can perpetuate or exacerbate these injustices. A key contribution is the introduction of the concept of "generative hermeneutical erasure," which describes how AI, especially when deployed across different cultures, can erode or displace local epistemologies by imposing a Western-centric "view from nowhere." The paper concludes by discussing potential remedies and directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by systematically organizing and applying the concept of epistemic injustice to the increasingly relevant field of AI.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The initial attempt to create a general taxonomy of epistemic injustices, drawing on diverse philosophical sources, offers a helpful starting point for analysis. The integration of Fricker's, Pohlhaus', Medina's, and Miragoli's perspectives is well-executed.
*   **Application to AI:** The extension of this taxonomy to AI is timely and relevant. The paper effectively demonstrates how various forms of epistemic injustice can manifest in AI systems, from algorithmic profiling to the epistemic opacity of machine learning models.
*   **Novel Concept:** The introduction of "generative hermeneutical erasure" is a genuinely novel contribution. It highlights a subtle but potentially far-reaching problem: the way AI, in its apparent neutrality, can systematically undermine non-Western epistemologies.
*   **Decolonial Perspective:** The incorporation of a decolonial perspective, emphasizing the Western bias inherent in many AI systems, adds depth and critical insight.
*   **Suggestions for Redress:** The discussion of potential remedies, including promoting inclusion, addressing design flaws, and fostering decolonial AI, is practical and forward-looking.
*   **Relating to AI Risks:** The mapping to the MIT AI Risk Repository provides a tangible link between philosophical concepts and practical risk management.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Speculative Nature:** The concept of "generative hermeneutical erasure," while insightful, remains somewhat speculative. The paper could benefit from more concrete examples and empirical evidence to support its claims.
*   **Scope Limitation:** The focus is primarily on Western vs. non-Western contexts. While this is a crucial area, other forms of cultural and social difference could be considered within the framework.
*   **Lack of Technical Detail:**  The paper could benefit from more engagement with the technical aspects of AI design and implementation. While it critiques Western biases, a deeper understanding of how these biases are encoded and reproduced in algorithms would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Overly Dense Language:** At times, the paper uses complex philosophical jargon that could alienate readers unfamiliar with the field. More effort to translate these concepts into accessible language would broaden its impact.
*   **Rule of Mono-Inheritance:** The attempt to simplify the taxonomy by enforcing "mono-inheritance" for concepts may limit its ability to capture the complexity of real-world situations, where different forms of injustice can overlap and interact.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's main strength lies in its systematic approach and the introduction of the "generative hermeneutical erasure" concept. While the existing literature acknowledges epistemic injustice in AI, this paper provides a more nuanced framework for understanding its various forms and offers a new lens for analyzing the cultural impact of AI. The paper's decolonial perspective is particularly valuable in the context of global AI development.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence research in AI ethics, particularly in the areas of fairness, accountability, and cultural sensitivity. It could also inform the design and deployment of AI systems in ways that are more respectful of diverse epistemologies. However, its impact will depend on its ability to engage with the technical AI community and translate its philosophical insights into practical guidelines.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates good novelty and significance. The paper's systematic approach and the introduction of "generative hermeneutical erasure" concept make it a significant paper worth considering. It also has influence on how fairness, accountability, and cultural sensitivity are approached in AI. However, the lack of concrete evidence for some speculative arguments as well as scope and technical limitations of the project, prevent the paper from receiving a higher rating. The complexity and length of the paper would have to be revised for it to be accessible to a broader audience.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### STeP: A General and Scalable Framework for Solving Video Inverse Problems with Spatiotemporal Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07549v1)
- **Authors**: Bingliang Zhang, Zihui Wu, Berthy T. Feng, Yang Song, Yisong Yue, Katherine L. Bouman
- **Abstract**: We study how to solve general Bayesian inverse problems involving videos using diffusion model priors. While it is desirable to use a video diffusion prior to effectively capture complex temporal relationships, due to the computational and data requirements of training such a model, prior work has instead relied on image diffusion priors on single frames combined with heuristics to enforce temporal consistency. However, these approaches struggle with faithfully recovering the underlying temporal relationships, particularly for tasks with high temporal uncertainty. In this paper, we demonstrate the feasibility of practical and accessible spatiotemporal diffusion priors by fine-tuning latent video diffusion models from pretrained image diffusion models using limited videos in specific domains. Leveraging this plug-and-play spatiotemporal diffusion prior, we introduce a general and scalable framework for solving video inverse problems. We then apply our framework to two challenging scientific video inverse problems--black hole imaging and dynamic MRI. Our framework enables the generation of diverse, high-fidelity video reconstructions that not only fit observations but also recover multi-modal solutions. By incorporating a spatiotemporal diffusion prior, we significantly improve our ability to capture complex temporal relationships in the data while also enhancing spatial fidelity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "STEP: A General and Scalable Framework for Solving Video Inverse Problems with Spatiotemporal Diffusion Priors":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STEP, a novel framework designed to solve video inverse problems by leveraging spatiotemporal diffusion priors. The approach addresses the challenge of limited video data by fine-tuning latent video diffusion models from pre-trained image diffusion models. This data-efficient strategy reduces computational costs, making spatiotemporal diffusion priors more accessible. STEP integrates these priors into a plug-and-play (PnP) inversion method, enabling the generation of high-fidelity, diverse video reconstructions. The framework is applied to two challenging scientific problems: black hole imaging and dynamic MRI, demonstrating its ability to capture complex temporal relationships and multi-modal solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *practical and accessible* implementation of spatiotemporal diffusion priors for video inverse problems. While video diffusion models exist, training them from scratch is computationally demanding. The key innovation is the fine-tuning approach, which substantially reduces the data and computational requirements. The use of a PnP framework (DAPS) is not entirely novel (as PnP methods are well-established), but the integration of the fine-tuned video diffusion prior *within* this framework is a significant contribution. The introduction of the joint image-video fine-tuning technique is also a valuable addition.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its ability to address complex video inverse problems, especially in scientific domains where data is limited and computational resources are constrained. The ability to generate multi-modal solutions is particularly important for ill-posed problems like black hole imaging, enabling a better understanding of uncertainty. The significant improvements in temporal consistency (quantified by the d-PSNR metric) demonstrated by the framework highlight its advantage over existing image-based diffusion priors with temporal heuristics. The applications chosen (black hole imaging, dynamic MRI) are both important and challenging, making the results impactful. The code release also contributes to the impact on the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data Efficiency:** The fine-tuning strategy makes spatiotemporal diffusion priors practical for problems with limited video data.
    *   **Improved Temporal Consistency:** STEP achieves significant improvements in temporal consistency compared to methods relying on image diffusion priors and heuristics.
    *   **Multi-modal Solution Recovery:** The framework is capable of generating diverse and equally plausible video reconstructions, addressing the ill-posed nature of many video inverse problems.
    *   **Generality and Scalability:** STEP can be applied to various video inverse problems, including non-linear forward models.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper clearly explains the methodology and presents convincing experimental results. The code is released, increasing replicability and impact.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Image Prior:** While the fine-tuning is clever, the reliance on a pre-trained *image* diffusion model *limits* the potential for learning fundamentally new spatiotemporal relationships. It's building upon a prior assumption that is spatially rich but temporally naive. While fine-tuning alleviates this, a true end-to-end training of a spatiotemporal model would likely yield even better results (although at a much higher cost).
    *   **Computational Cost (Sampling):**  While training is more efficient, the sampling process (as highlighted in the discussion) still requires backpropagation through the decoder, adding to the computational burden during inference. This needs to be addressed.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While two applications were presented, further exploration with other scientific video inverse problems would strengthen the generality claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by making spatiotemporal diffusion priors a more accessible and practical tool for solving video inverse problems. The fine-tuning strategy could be adopted in other contexts where training data is limited. The framework's ability to generate multi-modal solutions could also spur further research into uncertainty quantification in video reconstruction.

**Overall Score:**

Considering the novelty of the fine-tuning approach for enabling practical spatiotemporal diffusion priors, the significant improvements in temporal consistency, and the impact on challenging scientific problems, alongside the identified weaknesses, a score of **8** is appropriate. The work represents a valuable contribution to the field, significantly advancing the applicability of diffusion models to video inverse problems but there are some limitations mentioned above. While not a complete paradigm shift, the practical and efficient approach holds substantial promise for further development and adoption.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Using LLMs for Analyzing AIS Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07557v1)
- **Authors**: Gaspard Mertends, Gilles Dejaegere, Mahmoud Sakr
- **Abstract**: Recent research in Large Language Models (LLMs), has had a profound impact across various fields, including mobility data science. This paper explores the and experiment with different approaches to using LLMs for analyzing AIS data. We propose a set of carefully designed queries to assess the reasoning capabilities of LLMs in this kind of tasks. Further, we experiment with four different methods: (1) using LLMs as a natural language interface to a spatial database, (2) reasoning on raw data, (3) reasoning on compressed trajectories, and (4) reasoning on semantic trajectories. We investigate the strengths and weaknesses for the four methods, and discuss the findings. The goal is to provide valuable insights for both researchers and practitioners on selecting the most appropriate LLM-based method depending on their specific data analysis objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to the analysis of Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, a rich source of information about ship movements. The authors investigate four different methods: (1) using LLMs as a Natural Language Interface to a database (NLIDB), (2) zero-shot data analysis on raw AIS data, (3) zero-shot analysis on compressed trajectories, and (4) zero-shot analysis on semantic trajectories. They design a taxonomy of 27 queries, grouped into attribute, individual trajectory, interaction, and data-fusion categories to assess the reasoning capabilities of the LLMs.  The paper then compares the performance of these four methods across varying dataset sizes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and timely research question: how can LLMs be effectively used to analyze AIS data? The increasing availability and sophistication of LLMs makes this a worthwhile area of exploration. The paper's strength lies in its comparative analysis of different approaches. The authors systematically evaluate the NLIDB method against three zero-shot data analysis approaches, offering insights into the trade-offs between them. The query taxonomy is well-defined and attempts to move beyond simple "direct translation" GIS queries, which adds to the paper's contribution.

However, the paper has several limitations:

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the application of LLMs to AIS data is interesting, the core techniques – NLIDB and zero-shot analysis – are not particularly novel in themselves. The paper essentially applies existing methods to a new domain.
*   **Lack of Deep Technical Contribution:** The paper lacks a deep technical contribution in terms of new algorithms or models. It mainly focuses on empirical evaluation. The transformation into "semantic trajectories" is simplistic.
*   **Zero-Shot Focus:** The paper exclusively focuses on out-of-the-box LLM capabilities, using the Gemini-1.5-flash model. While this is a valid starting point, the paper does not explore the potential benefits of fine-tuning or few-shot learning, which could significantly improve performance. The justification for selecting Gemini solely based on context window size is also weak. Other factors such as model architecture and pre-training data influence performance significantly.
*   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on accuracy, neglecting other important metrics like efficiency, latency, and cost.  There is also minimal discussion of the failure cases, providing only a few examples. The ground-truth methodology seems reasonable but relies on Python/SQL scripts for most queries.
*   **Unsurprising Results:** The conclusion that NLIDB is more stable than ZSA methods for larger datasets is not surprising. It's expected that relying on pre-trained knowledge will be insufficient when data volumes increase. The result that raw data input works better than data transformation is interesting, but the transformations applied were not state-of-the-art. The experiment is therefore not conclusive.
*   **Limited Discussion:** The paper's discussion of related work could be expanded to better situate their research within the broader context of LLMs for geospatial analysis. Several more recent works leveraging fine-tuning and novel prompt engineering techniques are missing.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in providing a preliminary exploration of LLMs for AIS data analysis. It highlights the potential benefits and limitations of different approaches, which can inform future research directions.  The query taxonomy provides a useful starting point for benchmarking LLM performance on AIS data.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a modest contribution to the field by exploring the application of LLMs to AIS data analysis.  However, the lack of technical novelty, the limited scope of the evaluation, and the lack of a deeper exploration of LLM capabilities limit its overall impact. The paper provides a useful starting point for future research, but it does not represent a significant breakthrough. The paper therefore warrants a score of 5.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 5/10

### PhaseGen: A Diffusion-Based Approach for Complex-Valued MRI Data Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07560v1)
- **Authors**: Moritz Rempe, Fabian Hörst, Helmut Becker, Marco Schlimbach, Lukas Rotkopf, Kevin Kröninger, Jens Kleesiek
- **Abstract**: Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) raw data, or k-Space data, is complex-valued, containing both magnitude and phase information. However, clinical and existing Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based methods focus only on magnitude images, discarding the phase data despite its potential for downstream tasks, such as tumor segmentation and classification. In this work, we introduce $\textit{PhaseGen}$, a novel complex-valued diffusion model for generating synthetic MRI raw data conditioned on magnitude images, commonly used in clinical practice. This enables the creation of artificial complex-valued raw data, allowing pretraining for models that require k-Space information. We evaluate PhaseGen on two tasks: skull-stripping directly in k-Space and MRI reconstruction using the publicly available FastMRI dataset. Our results show that training with synthetic phase data significantly improves generalization for skull-stripping on real-world data, with an increased segmentation accuracy from $41.1\%$ to $80.1\%$, and enhances MRI reconstruction when combined with limited real-world data. This work presents a step forward in utilizing generative AI to bridge the gap between magnitude-based datasets and the complex-valued nature of MRI raw data. This approach allows researchers to leverage the vast amount of avaliable image domain data in combination with the information-rich k-Space data for more accurate and efficient diagnostic tasks. We make our code publicly $\href{https://github.com/TIO-IKIM/PhaseGen}{\text{available here}}$.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PhaseGen, a novel complex-valued diffusion model designed to generate synthetic MRI raw data (k-Space data) conditioned on magnitude images.  The motivation is that while a vast amount of MRI magnitude image data exists, datasets containing complex-valued raw data are scarce, limiting the application of AI models that leverage this richer information. PhaseGen aims to bridge this gap by generating artificial k-Space data, allowing researchers to pretrain models on synthetic data and then fine-tune on real-world data, reducing the reliance on limited real-world k-Space datasets. The authors evaluate PhaseGen on two tasks: skull-stripping directly in k-Space and MRI reconstruction using the FastMRI dataset.  Results demonstrate that training with PhaseGen-generated data improves generalization for skull-stripping (significantly increasing segmentation accuracy) and enhances MRI reconstruction performance when combined with limited real-world data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in applying diffusion models to generate *complex-valued* k-Space MRI data conditioned on magnitude images. While diffusion models are increasingly used in image generation, their application to the *frequency domain* of medical imaging and handling complex data is a valuable contribution. Previous uses of diffusion models in MRI have primarily focused on image reconstruction from undersampled data, not the generation of raw data itself. The complex number aspect brings a higher level of challenge, as previous works have focused more on 2D or 3D images within the image domain.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a key bottleneck in MRI research: the limited availability of complex-valued raw data. By creating synthetic data, PhaseGen unlocks the potential to leverage existing vast magnitude image datasets for training models that benefit from k-Space information (e.g., improved artifact handling, potentially more robust feature extraction). This could lead to better diagnostic accuracy and efficiency in MRI workflows. The observed improvement in generalization for skull-stripping on real-world data using PhaseGen-augmented training is particularly compelling, as it validates the model's ability to generate realistic and useful data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear Motivation and Problem Statement: The paper clearly articulates the problem of limited k-Space data and the potential benefits of using complex-valued information.
    *   Technically Sound Approach: The description of the complex-valued diffusion model is well-explained, and the adaptations necessary for handling phase generation are appropriately addressed.
    *   Strong Empirical Validation: The evaluation on two distinct tasks (skull-stripping and reconstruction) provides convincing evidence of PhaseGen's effectiveness. The dramatic increase in skull-stripping accuracy demonstrates the practical value of the generated data.
    *   Publicly Available Code: The availability of the code promotes reproducibility and further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited Real-World Validation for Reconstruction: While the paper demonstrates improved reconstruction performance with limited real data *combined* with synthetic data, it would be even stronger to show how *only* PhaseGen-generated data can improve on standard methods without any real-world k-space data. This scenario would better highlight the impact of PhaseGen as a pretraining tool.
    *   Lack of Comparison to Other Generative Models: It would be valuable to compare PhaseGen against other generative models (e.g., GANs, VAEs) adapted for complex-valued k-Space data. This could provide insights into the relative advantages and disadvantages of using diffusion models for this specific task.
    *   Specific to Single Coil Data: The paper states the model is trained only on single-coil data, and also mentions the need to expand this capability to multiple-coil acquisitions. This limits the generalizability and applicability of the current implementation.
    *   Limited Analysis of the Generated Phase: While results are better, the paper does not offer deep theoretical insights into the characteristics or quality of the generated phase information. It would strengthen the study to analyze the generated phase quantitatively against different types of artifacts and data corruptions.
    *   In the experiments, the proposed approach achieved better performance than naive methods and no-phase, however, it did not achieve same performance as the original one. This opens opportunity for further improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** PhaseGen has the potential to influence research directions in MRI by enabling the development of AI models that can leverage complex-valued raw data. The ability to generate realistic synthetic k-Space data can accelerate research in areas such as:
    *   Artifact Reduction: Training models to identify and remove artifacts directly in k-Space.
    *   Advanced Reconstruction Techniques: Developing more sophisticated reconstruction algorithms.
    *   Novel Diagnostic Applications: Discovering new biomarkers or features in k-Space that are not apparent in magnitude images.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5**. This is a solid paper that addresses a significant problem in MRI research with a technically sound and well-validated approach. The novelty of applying diffusion models to generate complex-valued k-Space data is noteworthy. The demonstrated improvements in skull-stripping accuracy and MRI reconstruction, even with limited real-world data, highlight the practical value of the work. The main weaknesses relate to limited comparisons, limited testing/experimentation and the lack of theoretical insights into the generated data. Further research addressing these limitations could significantly increase the impact of this work.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion Transformers for Tabular Data Time Series Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07566v1)
- **Authors**: Fabrizio Garuti, Enver Sangineto, Simone Luetto, Lorenzo Forni, Rita Cucchiara
- **Abstract**: Tabular data generation has recently attracted a growing interest due to its different application scenarios. However, generating time series of tabular data, where each element of the series depends on the others, remains a largely unexplored domain. This gap is probably due to the difficulty of jointly solving different problems, the main of which are the heterogeneity of tabular data (a problem common to non-time-dependent approaches) and the variable length of a time series. In this paper, we propose a Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) based approach for tabular data series generation. Inspired by the recent success of DiTs in image and video generation, we extend this framework to deal with heterogeneous data and variable-length sequences. Using extensive experiments on six datasets, we show that the proposed approach outperforms previous work by a large margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TabDiT, a novel approach for generating time series of tabular data. It addresses the challenges of tabular data generation, specifically the heterogeneity of data types (numerical and categorical) and the variable length of time series. TabDiT adapts the Diffusion Transformer (DiT) architecture, previously successful in image and video generation, to the tabular domain.  The method employs a Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) framework with a non-holistic Variational Autoencoder (VAE) for encoding tabular row embeddings.  A transformer-based denoising network combines these embeddings to model the time-dependent distribution. A variable-range decimal representation for numerical field values and an autoregressive VAE decoder further enhance the model.  The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of TabDiT through experiments on six public datasets, comparing it to existing methods and showing significant improvements in terms of data realism and diversity. The proposed approach is applicable to both conditional and unconditional generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty by adapting a relatively new architecture (DiT) to an entirely new problem domain. Specifically, the novelty comes from the way it handles variable length sequences of heterogeneous data. Prior methods either struggled with sequence length or required converting numerical data to categorical representations, often sacrificing accuracy and diversity.

*   **Significance:** The generation of synthetic tabular data time series is important for privacy preservation, data augmentation, and addressing data scarcity in many real-world applications (finance, healthcare, etc.).  TabDiT provides a valuable tool for these tasks, outperforming existing methods by a significant margin. The proposed evaluation metric, which extends single-row metrics to the time-series domain, is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results: TabDiT significantly outperforms existing baselines (including a well-tuned AR model), suggesting a superior ability to model the complex distributions of real-world tabular time series data. The ablation studies demonstrate the importance of each component of the proposed method.
    *   Addresses key challenges: The paper clearly identifies and addresses the main difficulties in generating tabular data time series: heterogeneity of data and variable length sequences.
    *   Sound technical approach: The use of DiT architecture allows to naturally handle sequences, while a LDM paradigm enhances realism and data diversity.
    *   Comprehensive experiments: The experiments are performed on a variety of datasets.
    *   Good writing and clarity: the paper clearly presents the technical details of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited comparisons: While TabDiT is compared with other methods, the number of other tabular data time series generation models is quite limited. The paper could have explored more deeply why existing single-row tabular data generation methods were not directly applicable, instead of only stating they couldn't deal with sequences.
    *   Computational resources: The experimental setup requires a substantial amount of computational power and long training times, which might limit the accessibility of the method for researchers with limited resources.

*   **Potential Influence:** TabDiT has the potential to become a strong baseline for future research on tabular data time series generation.  The approach of combining VAE-encoded row embeddings with a transformer-based denoising network could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The release of code and the proposed evaluation metrics further enhance its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by adapting a state-of-the-art diffusion model to the under-explored domain of tabular data time series generation. The comprehensive experimental results demonstrate the method's effectiveness in addressing the challenges associated with this task. While limited comparative baselines and high computational costs are drawbacks, TabDiT's novelty and potential impact warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring Human-Like Thinking in Search Simulations with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07570v1)
- **Authors**: Erhan Zhang, Xingzhu Wang, Peiyuan Gong, Zixuan Yang, Jiaxin Mao
- **Abstract**: Simulating user search behavior is a critical task in information retrieval, which can be employed for user behavior modeling, data augmentation, and system evaluation. Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have opened up new possibilities for generating human-like actions including querying, browsing, and clicking. In this work, we explore the integration of human-like thinking into search simulations by leveraging LLMs to simulate users' hidden cognitive processes. Specifically, given a search task and context, we prompt LLMs to first think like a human before executing the corresponding action. As existing search datasets do not include users' thought processes, we conducted a user study to collect a new dataset enriched with users' explicit thinking. We investigate the impact of incorporating such human-like thinking on simulation performance and apply supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to teach LLMs to emulate both human thinking and actions. Our experiments span two dimensions in leveraging LLMs for user simulation: (1) with or without explicit thinking, and (2) with or without fine-tuning on the thinking-augmented dataset. The results demonstrate the feasibility and potential of incorporating human-like thinking in user simulations, though performance improvements on some metrics remain modest. We believe this exploration provides new avenues and inspirations for advancing user behavior modeling in search simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to simulate human-like thinking in search simulations. The authors argue that incorporating cognitive processes into user behavior modeling can improve the realism and interpretability of simulations. They collected a new dataset containing user's explicit thinking alongside their search actions using a think-aloud protocol. They then fine-tuned LLMs on this dataset to emulate both human thinking and actions.  The paper investigates different approaches for leveraging LLMs, with and without explicit thinking, and with and without fine-tuning, and evaluates their impact on query generation, click prediction, and stopping behavior. The results show the feasibility of integrating human-like thinking into search simulations but note that performance improvements are modest.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating explicit human-like thinking (simulated through LLMs) into user search simulations is a worthwhile exploration. Prior work has used LLMs for user simulation, but this paper explicitly focuses on incorporating a think-aloud process and capturing those cognitive processes. Collecting and using a dataset enriched with user's verbalized thoughts is also a valuable contribution. This addresses a significant gap in many existing user behavior datasets. However, the methodology relies heavily on existing LLM frameworks (USimAgent, React) and fine-tuning techniques. The novelty lies primarily in the application of these methods to a new dataset and the specific problem of explicitly simulating cognitive processes.

*   **Significance:** User simulation is a vital tool for information retrieval research. Improving the realism of these simulations by incorporating cognitive aspects can lead to better system evaluation, more effective user behavior modeling, and potentially, more accurate data augmentation techniques. The paper provides an initial investigation into a potentially fruitful avenue of research. However, the modest performance improvements raise questions about the practical impact of the current implementation. The work does set a foundation and opens possibilities for future research. The released dataset could also be significant in driving the work further.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Explicit Focus on Cognitive Processes:** The paper distinguishes itself by attempting to model and simulate users' cognitive processes directly.
    *   **New Dataset:**  The collection of a think-aloud dataset is a significant contribution to the field, as such datasets are rare and valuable.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper conducts a systematic evaluation of different LLM-based approaches, providing insights into their strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Modest Performance Improvements:** The experimental results show only modest improvements in some metrics when incorporating simulated thinking. This suggests that the current approach may not fully capture the complexity of human cognition or that the evaluation metrics are not sensitive enough.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Frameworks:** The method relies heavily on existing LLM frameworks and techniques. There is no deep innovation on the approach.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The experimental tasks and datasets are specific, which may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis**: While the paper presents comparative results, it lacks a detailed error analysis. Understanding *why* certain methods fail or succeed in specific situations is crucial for future improvements. For example, delving deeper into which types of "thinking" are most influential on behavior would be highly valuable.

*   **Impact:**  The paper's impact will depend on how the dataset and the initial findings are leveraged by other researchers.  It provides a solid starting point for future investigations into cognitive user modeling with LLMs. The results underscore the need for further research to explore more effective ways to model and integrate cognitive processes into user simulations.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly attempting to model and simulate human-like thinking within user search simulations, backed by a new dataset collected through a think-aloud protocol. This addresses a clear gap in existing research. However, the limited performance gains and reliance on existing frameworks temper the overall impact. The impact on the field is still uncertain but has the potential to inspire more robust and meaningful cognitive user simulations. A score of 6 reflects the paper's solid contribution and potential, while acknowledging its limitations and the need for further research.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### REANIMATOR: Reanimate Retrieval Test Collections with Extracted and Synthetic Resources
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07584v1)
- **Authors**: Björn Engelmann, Fabian Haak, Philipp Schaer, Mani Erfanian Abdoust, Linus Netze, Meik Bittkowski
- **Abstract**: Retrieval test collections are essential for evaluating information retrieval systems, yet they often lack generalizability across tasks. To overcome this limitation, we introduce REANIMATOR, a versatile framework designed to enable the repurposing of existing test collections by enriching them with extracted and synthetic resources. REANIMATOR enhances test collections from PDF files by parsing full texts and machine-readable tables, as well as related contextual information. It then employs state-of-the-art large language models to produce synthetic relevance labels. Including an optional human-in-the-loop step can help validate the resources that have been extracted and generated.
